S^{H^{\prime}_{i}}_{(3,d_{i},\lambda_{i},\eta_{i})}(EP_{\lambda_{i}[d_{i}]}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{K^{H^{\prime}_{i}}(\mathfrak{n})}\varphi_{i,S_{0}}\mathcal{T}_{i}f_{S_{1}})\\
=O_{\epsilon}(|\mathfrak{n}|^{\frac{9}{2}d_{i}(d_{i}-1)+(4+\epsilon)d_{i}+5}\Gamma_{-1^{(d)},3}(\mathfrak{n}_{i})q_{S_{1}}^{A+B\kappa}).

Proof.

This is the same argument as Lemma
9.4.2
except that we use

\tr_{\pi^{S}}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{K^{\mathrm{GL}_{3}}(\mathfrak{n})}\leq C(\epsilon)|\mathfrak{n}|^{4+\epsilon}

from corollary 9.2 in
[
MS19
]
. Our
C(\epsilon)
here is the product of Marshall-Shin’s
C(\epsilon,q_{v})
for
q_{v}\leq q(\epsilon)
.
∎

9.4.4.
The full bound

Applying the previous results with
T_{i}=1,2,3
instead of directly applying Proposition
7.3.3
:

Corollary 9.4.4
.

The bound for

\Delta=(T_{i},d_{i},\lambda_{i},\eta_{i})_{1\leq i\leq k}\neq\Sigma_{\lambda,\eta}

in Theorem
9.3.2
may be tightened to

S^{G}_{\Delta}(\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda}f^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}})=O(|\mathfrak{n}|^{R(\Delta)}q_{S_{1}}^{A+B\kappa})

under all the same conditions and where

(9.4.1)

R(\Delta)=\bar{R}(\Delta)-\sum_{i:T_{i}=1}\left(\frac{1}{2}(d_{i}^{2}+d_{i})-1\right)-\sum_{i:T_{i}=2}\left(4d_{i}-(d_{i}+3)\right)\\
-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i:T_{i}=3\\
d_{i}>1\end{subarray}}\left(9d_{i}-((4+10^{-100})d_{i}+5)\right).

Proof.

For each summand of
\Delta
with
T_{i}=1
, let
H^{\prime}_{i}=H((1,d_{i},\lambda_{i},\eta_{i}))
. Then the method of proof of Theorem
9.3.2
implicitly bounds terms

S^{H^{\prime}_{i}}_{(1,d_{i},\lambda_{i},\eta_{i})}(\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda_{i}[d_{i}]}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{K^{H^{\prime}_{i}}(\mathfrak{n})}\varphi^{\prime}_{i,S_{0}}\mathcal{T}_{i}f_{S_{1}})=O(|\mathfrak{n}|^{\frac{1}{2}(d_{i}^{2}+d_{i})}\Gamma_{-1^{(d_{i}-1)},1^{(d_{i})}}(\mathfrak{n})q_{S_{1}}^{A+B\kappa}).

We instead use Lemma
9.4.1
to replace them by

S^{H^{\prime}_{i}}_{(1,d_{i},\lambda_{i},\eta_{i})}(EP_{\lambda_{i}[d_{i}]}f^{\infty})=O(|\mathfrak{n}|\Gamma_{1}(\mathfrak{n})q_{S_{1}}^{A+B\kappa}).

For summands of
\Delta
with
T_{i}=2
, we similarly use Lemma
9.4.2
to replace

O(|\mathfrak{n}|^{2(d_{i}^{2}+d_{i})}\Gamma_{-1^{(d_{i}-1)},2^{(d_{i})}}(\mathfrak{n})q_{S_{1}}^{A+B\kappa})\\
\mapsto O(|\mathfrak{n}|^{2d_{i}(d_{i}-1)+d_{i}+3}\Gamma_{-1^{(2d_{i}-2)},2}(\mathfrak{n})q_{S_{1}}^{A+B\kappa}).

For
T_{i}=3
, we use
9.4.3
to replace

O(|\mathfrak{n}|^{\frac{9}{2}(d_{i}^{2}+d_{i})}\Gamma_{-1^{(d_{i}-1)},3^{(d_{i})}}(\mathfrak{n})q_{S_{1}}^{A+B\kappa})\\
\mapsto O_{\epsilon}(|\mathfrak{n}|^{\frac{9}{2}d_{i}(d_{i}-1)+(4+10^{-100})d_{i}+5}\Gamma_{-1^{(d)},3}(\mathfrak{n})q_{S_{1}}^{A+B\kappa}).

Substituting in these stronger bounds produces the result.
4
4

4

Here, we were not very careful with the
\varphi_{S_{0}}
terms since we are not making claims about how the error term depends on them.

∎

Remark
.

The
R(\Delta)
is a better upper bound of the true growth rate than
\bar{R}(\Delta)
. It is obtained by
making three modifications to the dimension count of the parabolic:

•

When
T_{i}=1
, replace the dimension of the Borel in the
\mathrm{GL}_{d_{i}}
-block corresponding to that summand with
1
,

•

When
T_{i}=2
, replace the dimension of the Levi
\mathrm{GL}_{2}^{d_{i}}
in the
\mathrm{GL}_{2d_{i}}
-block corresponding to that summand with
d_{i}+3
,

•

When
T_{i}=3
, replace the dimension of the Levi
\mathrm{GL}_{3}^{d_{i}}
in the
\mathrm{GL}_{3d_{i}}
-block corresponding to that summand with
(4+\epsilon)d_{i}+5
.

The next section describes a case where
R(\Delta)
is the optimal growth rate.

9.5.
Odd GSK Shapes

Now that we understand
S_{\Sigma_{\lambda,\eta}}
, we can use Proposition
8.2.2
to compute the limiting asymptotics for odd Generalized Saito-Kurokawa (GSK) shapes (recall: these are shapes
\Delta=(\Delta_{i})_{i}=(T_{i},d_{i},\lambda_{i},\eta_{i})_{1\leq i\leq k}
such that
d_{1}=1
,
T_{i}=1
for
i\geq 2
, and the
d_{i}
are odd and distinct).
Keep the same setup as Section
9.1
. Additionally,
let
\Delta
as above be odd GSK with
H(\Delta)=G
and let
H_{i}=H(\Delta_{i})
.

We will now prove our second key technical bound, namely that
R(\Delta)
is the exact growth rate for
S^{G}_{\Delta}
.

Note
.

As stated before, the distinctness part of definition
8.2.1
of odd-GSK will not be necessary for Theorem
9.5.1
, only for our application in Section
11
. The non-GSK shapes to which
9.5.1
applies will never be the dominant contribution to counts of forms.

Theorem 9.5.1
.

Fix
f_{\mathfrak{n}}^{\infty}
and
G\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{ell}}(N)
as in Section
9.1
. Assume
\Delta
is odd GSK as in Definition
8.2.1
and that the pair
(\Delta,\varphi_{S_{0}})
satisfies Conjecture
6.4.1
.

Then there are constants
A,B,C,D,E
with
C\geq 1
depending only on
G
and
\Delta
such that whenever
|\mathfrak{n}|\geq Dq_{S_{1}}^{E\kappa}
,

|\mathfrak{n}|^{-R(\Delta)}\Gamma_{L(\Delta)}(\mathfrak{n})^{-1}S^{G}_{\Delta}(\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda}f_{\mathfrak{n}}^{\infty})\\
=2^{-k+1}\Lambda(H_{1},\mathcal{T}_{1}f_{S_{1}},\varphi_{1,S_{0}})\times\prodf_{i\geq 2}\Lambda^{\mathrm{ab}}(H_{i},\mathcal{T}_{i}f_{S_{1}},\varphi_{i,S_{0}})+O(|\mathfrak{n}|^{-C}q_{S_{1}}^{A+B\kappa}),

with growth rate from Corollary
9.4.4
(which simplifies in this case)

R(\Delta)=\frac{1}{2}\dim H_{1}+(k-1)+\frac{1}{2}\left(\dim G-\sum_{i\geq 2}\dim H_{i},\right),

indexing list

L(\Delta)=T_{1},1^{(k-1)},-1^{(k-1)},

and masses

\displaystyle\Lambda(H_{1},\mathcal{T}_{1}f_{S_{1}},\varphi_{1,S_{0}})

\displaystyle=\varphi_{1,S_{0}}(1)(\mathcal{T}_{1}f_{S_{1}})(1)\frac{\dim\lambda_{1}}{|\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(\lambda_{1})|}\frac{\vol(H_{1}(F)\backslash H_{1}(\mathbb{A}_{F}))}{\vol(K_{H_{1}}^{S})\vol(H^{c}_{1,\infty})},

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\mathrm{ab}}(H_{i},\mathcal{T}_{i}f_{S_{1}},\varphi_{i,S_{0}})

\displaystyle=\frac{\vol(H_{i}(F)^{\mathrm{ab}}\backslash H_{i}(\mathbb{A})^{\mathrm{ab}})}{\vol(K_{H_{i}^{\mathrm{ab}}}^{S})\vol((H_{i,\infty})^{\mathrm{ab}})}\int_{H_{i,\mathrm{der},S_{0},S_{1}}}\mathcal{T}_{i}f_{S_{1}}\varphi_{i,S_{0}}(h)\,dh.

(Recall the
\mathcal{T}_{i}f_{S_{1}}
and
\varphi_{i,S_{0}}
are defined as in Lemma
6.2.2
and Conjecture
6.4.1
.)

Proof.

We first apply Proposition
8.2.2
with
S_{b}=S_{0}
and
S_{s}
the places dividing
\mathfrak{n}
: whenever
|\mathfrak{n}|
is big enough,

S^{G}_{\Delta}(\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda}f_{\mathfrak{n}}^{\infty})=2^{-k+1}I(\mathfrak{n})\prodf_{i}S^{H_{i}}_{(T_{i},d_{i},\lambda_{i},\eta_{i})}(EP_{\lambda_{i}[d_{i}]}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{K^{H_{i}}(\mathfrak{n})}\varphi_{i,S_{0}}\mathcal{T}_{i}f_{S_{1}}),

where we applied Lemma
9.2.1
to compute constant terms.

For
i=1
,
d_{i}=1
so from Theorem
9.3.2
we get (for each summand in the
\prodf
):

S^{H_{1}}_{(T_{1},1,\lambda_{1},\eta_{1})}(EP_{\lambda_{1}[d_{1}]}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{K^{H_{1}}(\mathfrak{n})}\varphi_{1,S_{0}}\mathcal{T}_{1}f_{S_{1}})\\
=|\mathfrak{n}|^{\dim H_{1}}\Gamma_{T_{1}}(\mathfrak{n})\Lambda(H_{1},\mathcal{T}_{1}f_{S_{1}},\varphi_{1,S_{0}})+O(|\mathfrak{n}|^{\dim H_{1}-C}q_{S_{1}}^{A+B\kappa}).

For
i>1
,
T_{i}=1
, so we apply Lemma
9.4.1
and get:

S^{H_{i}}_{(1,d_{i},\lambda_{i},\eta_{i})}(EP_{\lambda_{i}[d_{i}]}f^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}})\\
=|\mathfrak{n}|\Gamma_{1}(\mathfrak{n})\frac{\vol(H_{i}(F)^{\mathrm{ab}}\backslash H_{i}(\mathbb{A})^{\mathrm{ab}})}{\vol(K^{H_{i}^{\mathrm{ab}},S})\vol((H_{i,\infty})^{\mathrm{ab}})}\int_{H_{i,\mathrm{der},S_{1},S_{0}}}\mathcal{T}_{i}f_{S_{1}}\varphi_{i,S_{0}}(h)\,dh.

The result follows from multiplying and summing over factorizable summands in the
\prodf
and taking the maximum over the various
A
’s through
E
’s above. We use the second part of Lemma
9.2.1
to estimate
I(\mathfrak{n}_{i})
and note that the
\dim G/P
there is
1/2(\dim G-\dim M)
.
∎

Note
.

We write the scaling factor in the theorem statement as it is to emphasize the exact growth in
\mathfrak{n}
. It comes from the more conceptual formula:

|\mathfrak{n}|^{-R(\Delta)}\Gamma_{L(\Delta)}(\mathfrak{n})=\left(|\mathfrak{n}|^{\frac{1}{2}(\dim G-\sum_{i}\dim H_{i})}\Gamma_{-1}(\mathfrak{n})^{k-1}\right)\left(\prod_{i}[K^{\mathrm{GL}_{T_{i}}}:K^{\mathrm{GL}_{T_{i}}}(\mathfrak{n})]\right)

where the first factor comes from the parabolic descent of the function
\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{K(\mathfrak{n})}
and the second from the expected growth rates of counts on the groups
H_{i}
.

We give two examples with
S_{0}=\emptyset
, removing dependence on Conjecture
6.4.1
.

Example
.

Consider the case when
S_{1}=S_{0}=\emptyset
. Then the theorem reduces to

(9.5.1)

|\mathfrak{n}|^{-R(\Delta)}\Gamma_{L(\Delta)}(\mathfrak{n})S^{G}_{\Delta}(\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda}f_{\mathfrak{n}}^{\infty})\\
=2^{-k+1}\frac{\dim\lambda_{1}}{|\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(\lambda_{1})|}\frac{\vol(H(F)\backslash H(\mathbb{A}_{F}))}{\vol(K_{H}^{\infty})\vol(H^{c}_{\infty})}+O(|\mathfrak{n}|^{-C})

where

H=H_{1}\times\prod_{i>1}H_{i}^{\mathrm{ab}}.

Example
.

Consider the case when
S_{0}=\emptyset
and
S_{1}
is a singleton
\{v\}
. Let
\mu^{\mathrm{pl},\mathrm{ur}}(H_{v})
be the Plancherel measure on the unramified spectrum of
\widehat{H}_{v}
where

H=H_{1}\times\prod_{i>1}H_{i}^{\mathrm{ab}}

as before. Then
H
is dual to the group associated to
\Delta
in (
6.2.2
) and comes with a map on the space of Satake parameters as in (
6.2.3
):

\mathcal{S}_{\Delta}:\widehat{H}^{\mathrm{ur},\mathrm{temp}}_{v}\to\widehat{G}^{\mathrm{ur}}_{v}.

Define the pushforward

\mu^{\mathrm{pl}(\Delta),\mathrm{ur}}_{v}:=\mu^{\mathrm{pl}(\Delta),\mathrm{ur}}(G_{v}):=(\mathcal{S}_{\Delta})_{*}(\mu^{\mathrm{pl},\mathrm{ur}}(H_{v})).

By (
6.2.4
), the factors related to
f_{S_{1}}
in the
\Lambda
’s from Theorem
9.5.1
multiply to

(\mathcal{T}_{\Delta}f_{v})(1)=\mu^{\Delta}_{v}(\widehat{f}_{v})

by Fourier inversion. Substituting this into
9.5.1
, we get

(9.5.2)

|\mathfrak{n}|^{-R(\Delta)}\Gamma_{L(\Delta)}(\mathfrak{n})S^{G}_{\Delta}(\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda}f_{\mathfrak{n}}^{\infty})\\
=\left(2^{-k+1}\frac{\dim\lambda_{1}}{|\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(\lambda_{1})|}\frac{\vol(H(F)\backslash H(\mathbb{A}_{F}))}{\vol(K_{H}^{\infty})\vol(H^{c}_{\infty})}\right)\mu^{\mathrm{pl}(\Delta),\mathrm{ur}}_{v}(\widehat{f}_{v})+O(|\mathfrak{n}|^{-C}q_{S_{1}}^{A+B\kappa}).

This will be used to interpret main Theorem
11.4.1
as Plancherel equidistribution.

9.6.
General Shapes: Conjectural Optimal Bound

Considerations of the notion of GK-dimension give us a heuristic for an optimal growth rate for any
\Delta
.

For
\pi_{v}
a representation of a
p
-adic group
G_{v}
, the Harish-Chandra-Howe local character expansion gives an expression

\Theta_{\pi_{v}}(\exp g)=\sum_{O\in N}c_{O}(\pi)\widehat{\mu}_{O}(g)

where
N
is the set of nilpotent orbits of
G_{v}
acting on
\Lie G
,
\mu_{O}(G)
is the Fourier transform of the
\delta
-measure on the orbit
O
,
c_{O}(\pi)
are constants, and
g\in\Lie G
is in a small enough open compact at the identity.

Definition 9.6.1
.

With the notation as above, let the GK-dimension of
\pi_{v}

d_{GK}(\pi_{v}):=\frac{1}{2}\max\{\dim O:c_{O}(\pi)\neq 0\}.

We can then compute:

Lemma 9.6.2
.

Assume
G_{v}
is unramified. Then

\dim\left(\pi_{v}^{K^{G}(q_{v}^{n})}\right)=\tr_{\pi_{v}}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{K^{G}(q_{v}^{n})}\asymp q_{v}^{nd_{GK}(n)}.

Mœglin and Walspurger in
[
MW87
]
associate to each
O\in N
a particular “degenerate Whittaker model”
W_{O}
. They prove that the maximal
O
such that
c_{O}(\pi)\neq 0
are exactly the same as the maximal
O
such that
\Hom(\pi_{v},W_{O})\neq 0
.

Now specialize to
G_{v}=\mathrm{GL}_{n}(F_{v})
. For
\pi_{v}
a tempered representation of
\mathrm{GL}_{t}(F_{v})
define
\pi_{v}[d]
to be the Langlands quotient of the parabolic induction

\Ind_{P}^{G_{v}}(\pi_{v}|\det|^{(d-1)/2}\boxtimes\pi_{v}|\det|^{(d-3)/2}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes\pi_{v}|\det|^{-(d-1)/2}).

This is the local component of the Speh representations
\pi_{\tau[d]}
in §
2.2.1
.

Any tempered
\pi_{v}
on
\mathrm{GL}_{t}(F_{v})
is generic and therefore satisfies

GK(\pi_{v})=\frac{1}{2}t(t-1).

On the other hand,
[
MW87
]
compute (see
[
Mit20
]
for a summary) that the maximal
O\in N
such that
\Hom(\pi_{v}[d],W_{O})\neq 0
is the principal nilpotent orbit corresponding to the partition
(t^{(d)})
through Jordan normal form. We can therefore compute

GK(\pi_{v}[d])=\frac{1}{2}(t^{2}d^{2}-td^{2})=\frac{1}{2}d^{2}t(t-1)

and get

(9.6.1)

\dim\left(\pi_{v}[d]^{K(q_{v}^{n})}\right)\asymp q_{v}^{\frac{1}{2}t(t-1)(d^{2}-1)n}\dim\left(\pi_{v}^{K(q_{v}^{n})}\right).

By the Ramanujan conjecture, for any simple parameter
\psi[d]
, we expect all the
\psi_{v}
to correspond to tempered representations on the
\mathrm{GL}
side (this is in fact known for our case, see lemma 6.1 in
[
MS19
]
). Therefore we can use the heuristic (
9.6.1
) instead of Lemmas
9.4.1
,
9.4.2
,and
9.4.3
in Theorem
9.4.4
and get:

Conjecture 9.6.3
.

The bound for any

\Delta=(T_{i},d_{i},\lambda_{i},\eta_{i})_{1\leq i\leq k}\neq\Sigma_{\lambda,\eta}

in Theorem
9.3.2
may be tightened to

S^{|G|}_{\Delta}(\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda}f^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}})=O(|\mathfrak{n}|^{R_{0}(\Delta)}q_{S_{1}}^{A+B\kappa})

under all the same conditions and where:

\displaystyle R_{0}(\Delta)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\left(N^{2}-\sum_{i}T_{i}^{2}d_{i}^{2}\right)+\sum_{i}\left(T_{i}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}T_{i}(T_{i}-1)(d_{i}^{2}-1)\right)

\displaystyle=\bar{R}(\Delta)-\sum_{i}\left(\frac{1}{2}T_{i}^{2}d_{i}(d_{i}+1)-\left(T_{i}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}T_{i}(T_{i}-1)(d_{i}^{2}-1)\right)\right).

We think of
R_{0}(\Delta)
again as making a modification to the dimension count of the parabolic that gives
\bar{R}(\Delta)
: replace the dimension of the parabolic corresponding to partition
(d_{i}^{(t_{i})})
in the
\mathrm{GL}_{t_{i}d_{i}}
-block on the diagonal with
T_{i}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}T_{i}(T_{i}-1)(d_{i}^{2}-1)
.

The main obstacle to proving Conjecture
9.6.3
is showing that the asymptotic (
9.6.1
) is uniform enough in
\pi_{v}
. This would require uniform upper bounds on the coefficients
c_{O}(\pi_{v})
for tempered
\pi_{v}
. In particular, we would need bounds for
non-maximal

O
in the wavefront set of
\pi_{v}
so the techniques of
[
MW87
]
don’t apply.

Of course, Conjecture
9.6.3
can only be exact for
S^{|G|}_{\Delta}
instead of
S^{G}_{\Delta}
. In the case where all the
d_{i}
have the same parity,
s_{\psi}=1
for all
\psi\in\Delta
so these agree. Otherwise, the terms
S_{\psi}
for different
\psi\in\Delta
are attached to a varying sign
\epsilon_{\psi}(s_{\psi})
. If we naïvely assume some non-trivial cancellation, we get the following:

Conjecture 9.6.4
.

Recall the setup and conditions for Theorems
9.3.2
,
9.4.4
, and Conjecture
9.6.3
. Then, if all the
d_{i}
have the same parity,

C_{\epsilon,1}|\mathfrak{n}|^{R_{0}(\Delta)-\epsilon}\leq S^{G}_{\Delta}(\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda}f^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}})\leq C_{\epsilon,2}|\mathfrak{n}|^{R_{0}(\Delta)+\epsilon}

for all
\epsilon>0
and some constants
C_{\epsilon,1},C_{\epsilon,2}
such that
C_{\epsilon,2}=O_{\epsilon}(q_{S_{1}}^{A+B\kappa})
.

If the
d_{i}
have different parities, then

S^{G}_{\Delta}(\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda}f^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}})=o(|\mathfrak{n}|^{R_{0}(\Delta)}q_{S_{1}}^{A+B\kappa}).

10.
Application to Limit Multiplicities

Let
G
be an extended pure inner form of
G^{*}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{ell}}(N)
and fix a cohomological representation
\pi_{0}
of
G_{\infty}
. Fix
f^{\infty}
unramified outside of a finite set of finite places
S
. In this section, we use the stabilization of the trace formula to estimate

m^{G}(\pi_{0},f^{\infty}):=\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{AR}_{\mathrm{disc}}(G)}m_{\pi}\mathbf{1}_{\pi_{\infty}=\pi_{0}}\tr_{\pi^{\infty}}(f^{\infty})

in terms of the bound on terms
S^{H}_{\Delta^{\prime}}(\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda}(f^{\infty})^{\prime})
from §
9
.

10.1.
Preliminaries

Let
\psi_{\infty}(\pi_{0})
be the Arthur parameters
\psi_{\infty}
at infinity such that
\pi_{0}\in\Pi^{G}_{\psi_{\infty}}
. This is a finite (possibly empty) set since all such Arthur parameters share an infinitesimal character. For each
\psi_{\infty}\in\psi_{\infty}(\pi_{0})
, there is a finite number of
\Delta
such that
\psi\in\Delta
have infinite component
\psi_{\infty}
as in Lemma
4.3.4
—intuitively, these are parameterized by ways to group together simple factors of
\psi_{\infty}
that share the same Arthur-
\mathrm{SL}_{2}
.

In total, we get a finite (possibly empty) set of shapes
\Delta(\pi_{0})
such that

(10.1.1)

\pi_{0}\otimes\pi^{\infty}\in\Pi_{\psi}\implies\psi\in\Delta\text{ for some }\Delta\in\Delta(\pi_{0}).

The spectral decomposition then produces

(10.1.2)

m^{G}(\pi_{0},f^{\infty})=\sum_{\Delta\in\Delta(\pi_{0})}\sum_{\psi\in\Delta}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{\psi}}m^{\psi}_{\pi}\mathbf{1}_{\pi_{\infty}=\pi_{0}}\tr_{\pi^{\infty}}(f^{\infty}).

Specializing to a single
\Delta\in\Delta(\pi_{0})
, consider for any test function,
f_{\infty}
on
G_{\infty}
:

(10.1.3)

I_{\Delta}^{G}(f_{\infty}f^{\infty}):=\sum_{\psi\in\Delta}I^{G}_{\psi}(f_{\infty}f^{\infty})=\sum_{\psi\in\Delta}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{\psi}}m^{\psi}_{\pi}\tr_{\pi_{\infty}}(f_{\infty})\tr_{\pi^{\infty}}(f^{\infty}).

Applying Lemma
3.4.3
part (1) to (
10.1.3
) and comparing to (
10.1.2
) then gives:

Corollary 10.1.1
.

Let
\pi_{d}
be a discrete series representation appearing in the character formula for
\pi_{0}
with sign
\sigma
. Then:

m^{G}(\pi_{0},f^{\infty})=\sigma\sum_{\Delta\in\Delta(\pi_{0})}I^{G}_{\Delta}(\varphi_{\pi_{d}}f^{\infty}).

10.2.
Stabilization

Now we compute the
I_{\Delta}^{G}
by stabilizing. We begin with a more conceptual formula.

Let
H\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)
. Then
H=H_{1}\times H_{2}
for
H_{i}
quasisplit unitary groups. Therefore, we can abuse notation and use each
H_{i}
to also denote a representative in some
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{ell}}(N_{i})
that is isomorphic as an algebraic group. Any shape
\Delta
on
H
corresponds to a finite and possibly empty set of shapes
\Delta_{1}\times\Delta_{2}
on
H_{1}\times H_{2}
that push forward to
\Delta
. Stabilization of each
I^{G}_{\psi}
for
\psi\in\Delta
(through Theorem
2.6.5
) gives:

Proposition 10.2.1
.

Let
\pi_{d}
be a discrete series representation appearing in the character formula for
\pi_{0}
with sign
\sigma
. Then:

m^{G}(\pi_{0},f^{\infty})=\sigma\sum_{\Delta\in\Delta(\pi_{0})}\sum_{H_{1}\times H_{2}\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)}\\
\sum_{\Delta_{1}\times\Delta_{2}}\iota(G,H_{1}\times H_{2})\prodf_{i=1,2}S^{H_{i}}_{\Delta_{i}}((\varphi_{\pi_{d}})^{H_{i}}(f^{\infty})^{H_{i}}),

where the
\star^{H_{i}}
terms represent the corresponding factors of transfers to
H
.

Each
(\varphi_{\pi_{d}})^{H_{i}}
can be chosen to be a linear combination of EP-functions by standard formulas for transfers of pseudocoefficients. Explicitly, consider a pair
(H(s),\Delta^{\prime}(s))
that pushes forward to
\Delta
and the corresponding
s\in\mathcal{S}_{\Delta}
. Then

\tr_{\psi_{\infty}^{\Delta^{\prime}}}(\varphi_{\pi_{d}}^{H})=\eta^{\psi_{\infty}^{\Delta}}_{\pi_{0}}(s^{\prime}s_{\Delta})=\sigma\eta^{\psi_{\infty}^{\Delta}}_{\pi_{0}}(s^{\prime})

by the endoscopic character identity
2.6.2
where
s^{\prime}
is the lift of
s
to
S^{\natural}_{\Delta}
therein. For the second equality, we use
\eta^{\psi_{\infty}^{\Delta}}_{\pi_{0}}(s_{\Delta})=\sigma
as in the proof of part 2 of Lemma
3.4.3
. If
\Delta^{\prime}
has total infinitesimal character
\lambda
, then this gives

\tr_{\psi_{\infty}^{\Delta^{\prime}}}(\varphi_{\pi_{d}}^{H})=\sigma\eta^{\psi_{\infty}^{\Delta}}_{\pi_{0}}(s^{\prime})\tr_{\psi_{\infty}^{\Delta}}(\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda}),

since the EP-function trace is just
1
. Changing notation a bit produces:

Corollary 10.2.2
.

Let
G
be an extended pure inner form of
G^{*}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{ell}}(N)
and fix a cohomological representation
\pi_{0}
of
G_{\infty}
. Then

\displaystyle m^{G}(\pi_{0},f^{\infty})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\Delta\in\Delta(\pi_{0})}m^{G}(\pi_{0},\Delta,f^{\infty})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\Delta\in\Delta(\pi_{0})}\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}_{\Delta}}\iota(G,H(s))\eta^{\psi_{\infty}^{\Delta}}_{\pi_{0}}(s^{\prime})\sum_{\Delta_{1}\times\Delta_{2}}\prodf_{i=1,2}S^{H_{i}(s)}_{\Delta_{i}}(\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda_{i}}(f^{\infty})^{H_{i}}),

where
(H(s),\Delta^{\prime}(s))
is the pair of group and shape corresponding to
s\in\mathcal{S}_{\Delta}
which is lifted to
s^{\prime}\in S^{\natural}_{\Delta}
as in Theorem
2.6.2
,
\Delta_{1}\times\Delta_{2}
ranges over shapes on
H(s)=H_{1}(s)\times H_{2}(s)
such that the pair
(H(s),\Delta_{1}\times\Delta_{2})
is equivalent to
(H(s),\Delta^{\prime}(s))
, and
\lambda_{i}
is the total infinitesimal character of
\Delta_{i}
.

Note
.

The sum over
\Delta_{1}\times\Delta_{2}
will be a singleton unless
H_{1}\cong H_{2}
in which case there will be two terms that differ by transposing the factors.

Note
.

We briefly discuss the relation between this and the formulas in
[
Lab11
]
for transfers of pseudocoefficients. For simplicity, assume we are in a case where we never have
H_{1}\cong H_{2}
.

Then, for each
\Delta
there is only ever one possible choice
\Delta_{1}\times\Delta_{2}
due to the fixing of infinitesimal characters at infinity in shapes. The sum over EP-functions in Labesse’s formulas then comes from the sum over
\Delta(\pi_{0})
—these then correspond to different
\Delta_{1}\times\Delta_{2}
with non-conjugate infinitesimal characters.

Next, while different
\Delta\in\Delta(\pi_{0})
may have the same
\psi_{\infty}(\Delta)
or even
\mathcal{S}_{\Delta}
, the embeddings
\mathcal{S}_{\Delta}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{S}_{\psi_{\infty}^{\Delta}}
will differ. This accounts for unexpected differences in signs of coefficients of EP-functions in Labesse’s formulas.

10.3.
Transfer Factors

We eventually want to apply Proposition
10.2.2
to
f^{\infty}
as in §
9.1
. In the case where
S_{0}=\emptyset
, we will need to compute explicit endoscopic transfers at all places so we need to choose explicit local transfer factors that are consistent globally.

First, pick a global Whittaker datum
\omega
on the quasisplit form
G^{*}
of
G
. Since we are assuming that
S_{0}=\emptyset
, the group
G
is unramified at all finite places. Therefore
G^{*}_{v}=G_{v}
for all finite
v
and is in particular also unramified. This implies that
G^{*}
can be defined over
\mathcal{O}_{F}
so we can choose
\omega
so that the induced local data
\omega_{v}
are unramified/admissible everywhere as in
[
Hal93
, §7]
. This allows us to use the fundamental lemma for each
G_{v}
, though beware that we need to choose hyperspecial subgroups
K_{v}
consistent with
\omega_{v}
.

Next,
[
Kal18
, §4.4]
shows that the choice of
\omega
also gives us compatible local transfer factors on
G
itself (we note that
G
has simply connected derived subgroup to make the extra term in Theorem 4.4.1 disappear). At finite places
G_{v}
, the local factors stay the same as for the
G^{*}_{v}
.

Finally, to explicitly pin down characters at infinity in §
11.3
, we pick our global Whittaker datum consistent with
[
MR19
, Rmq 4.5]
(we can pick it consistent with any Whittaker datum up to equivalence for
G_{\infty}
since
E/F
is CM and
G^{*}
is quasisplit).

10.4.
Limit Multiplicities

As a preliminary/example computation, we work out what the summand
m^{G}(\pi_{0},\Delta,f^{\infty})
from Proposition
10.2.2
is for

\Delta=(T_{1},1,\lambda_{1},\eta_{1}),(1,d_{2},\lambda_{2},\eta_{2})

with
d_{2}
odd so that
\Delta
is odd GSK. In our eventual application, this will be the dominant term in the sum.

For this
\Delta
, we have
\mathcal{S}_{\Delta}\cong\mathbb{Z}/2
and the non-identity element
s
corresponds to

H(s)=H_{1}\times H_{2}=U(T_{1})\times U(d_{2}).

If
T_{1}\neq d_{2}
, there is a unique choice

\Delta_{1}\times\Delta_{2}=(T_{1},1,\lambda_{1},\eta_{1})\times(1,d_{2},\lambda_{2},\eta_{2})

and
\iota(G,H(s))=1/2
. If
T_{1}=d_{2}
, there are two choices for
\Delta_{1}\times\Delta_{2}
that correspond to the exact same product of
S
-terms and
\iota(G,H(s))=1/4
. Either way,
10.2.2
reduces to

(10.4.1)

m^{G}(\pi_{0},\Delta,f^{\infty})=S_{\Delta}^{G^{*}}(\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda}(f^{\infty})^{G^{*}})\\
+\frac{1}{2}\eta_{\pi_{0}}^{\psi^{\Delta}_{\infty}}(s^{\prime})S^{U(T_{1})}_{(T_{1},1,\lambda_{1},\eta_{2})}(\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda_{1}}(f^{\infty})^{U(T_{1})})\times S^{U(d_{2})}_{(1,d_{2},\lambda_{2},\eta_{2})}(\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda_{2}}(f^{\infty})^{U(d_{2})}),

where
G^{*}
is the quasisplit form of
G
and where the product implicitly includes a sum over factorizable summands of the transfer to
H(s)
.

Now assume

f^{\infty}:=f^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}}=\varphi_{S_{0}}f_{S_{1}}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{K^{G,S}(\mathfrak{n})}

is of the form in Section
9.1
. Furthermore, assume that the chosen transfers of
\varphi_{S_{0}}
satisfy Conjecture
6.4.1
for
\Delta
.

Then, by Theorem
9.5.1
, the first summand of (
10.4.1
) has main term:

\frac{1}{2}|\mathfrak{n}_{i}|^{\frac{1}{2}(N^{2}+T_{1}^{2}-d_{2}^{2})+1}\Gamma_{T_{1},-1,1}(\mathfrak{n}_{i})\frac{\dim\lambda_{1}}{|\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(\lambda_{1})|}\frac{\vol(H^{\prime}(F)\backslash H^{\prime}(\mathbb{A}_{f}))}{\vol(K^{S}_{H^{\prime}})\vol((H^{\prime}_{\infty})^{c})}\\
\varphi_{1,S_{0}}(1)f_{S_{1}}^{H_{1}}(1)\int_{H_{2,\mathrm{der},S_{0},S_{1}}}f^{H_{2}}_{S_{1}}\varphi_{2,S_{0}}(h)\,dh,

where
H^{\prime}=H_{1}\times H_{2}^{\mathrm{ab}}
. We use here that being unramified makes
\mathcal{T}_{i}f_{S_{1}}=f^{H_{i}}_{S_{1}}
.

Next, considerations as in Lemma
6.1.2
give that the transfer of the
\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{K^{G,S}(\mathfrak{n})}
term is a constant term to a Levi, so Lemma
9.2.1
gives

\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{K^{G,S}(\mathfrak{n})}^{H}=I(\mathfrak{n})\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{K^{H_{1},S}(\mathfrak{n})}\times\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{K^{H_{2},S}(\mathfrak{n})}.

We can therefore use Lemma
9.2.1
computing endoscopic transfers to get that the second summand of (
10.4.1
) has main term:

(10.4.2)

\frac{1}{2}|\mathfrak{n}|^{\frac{1}{2}(N^{2}-T_{1}^{2}-d_{2}^{2})}\Gamma_{-1}(\mathfrak{n})\eta_{\pi_{0}}^{\psi^{\Delta}_{\infty}}(s^{\prime})S^{H_{1}}_{(T_{1},1,\lambda_{1},\eta_{2})}(\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda_{1}}\varphi^{H_{1}}_{S_{0}}f^{H_{1}}_{S_{1}}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{K^{H_{1},S}(\mathfrak{n})})\times\\
S^{H_{2}}_{(1,d_{2},\lambda_{2},\eta_{2})}(\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda_{2}}\varphi^{H_{2}}_{S_{0}}f^{H_{2}}_{S_{1}}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{K^{H_{2},S}(\mathfrak{n})}).

Theorem
9.3.2
then gives that the first
s^{H}
factor in (
10.4.2
) has main term

|\mathfrak{n}|^{T_{1}^{2}}\Gamma_{T_{i}}(\mathfrak{n})\varphi^{H_{1}}_{S_{0}}(1)f_{S_{1}}^{H_{1}}(1)\frac{\vol(H_{1}(F)\backslash H_{1}(\mathbb{A}_{f}))}{\vol(K^{S}_{H_{1}})\vol(H^{c}_{1,\infty})}

and by Proposition
8.1.2
, the second
S^{H}
factor in (
10.4.2
) is eventually

|\mathfrak{n}|\Gamma_{1}(\mathfrak{n})\frac{\vol(H^{\mathrm{ab}}_{2}(F)\backslash H^{\mathrm{ab}}_{2}(\mathbb{A}_{f}))}{\vol(K_{H^{\mathrm{ab}}_{2}}^{S})\vol(H^{\mathrm{ab}}_{2,\infty})}\int_{H_{2,\mathrm{der},S_{0},S_{1}}}f^{H_{2}}_{S_{1}}\varphi^{H_{2}}_{S_{0}}(h)\,dh

after using an argument as in Corollary
9.4.4
to remove all the terms in the sum except for
1
.

After multiplying everything together, this shows that the summands for
G
and
H_{1}\times H_{2}
in (
10.4.1
) have the exact same asymptotic dependence on
\mathfrak{n}_{i}
.

When
S_{0}=\emptyset
, we no longer need Conjecture
6.4.1
and can collect these terms reasonably cleanly: for some
A,B,C,D,E
with
C\geq 1
, as long as
|\mathfrak{n}|\geq Dq_{S_{1}}^{E\kappa}
:

(10.4.3)

|\mathfrak{n}|^{-\frac{1}{2}(N^{2}+T_{1}^{2}-d_{2}^{2})-1}\Gamma_{T_{1},-1,1}(\mathfrak{n}_{i})^{-1}m^{G}(\pi_{0},\Delta,f_{S_{1}}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{K^{G,S}(\mathfrak{n})})\\
=\mathbf{1}_{\eta_{\pi_{0}}^{\psi^{\Delta}_{\infty}}(\mathcal{S}_{\Delta})=1}\frac{\dim\lambda_{1}}{|\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(\lambda_{1})|}\frac{\vol(H^{\prime}(F)\backslash H^{\prime}(\mathbb{A}_{f}))}{\vol(K^{S}_{H^{\prime}})\vol((H^{\prime}_{\infty})^{c})}\left(f_{S_{1}}^{H_{1}}(1)\int_{H_{2,\mathrm{der},S_{1}}}f^{H_{2}}_{S_{1}}(h)\,dh\right)\\
+O(|\mathfrak{n}|^{-C}q_{S_{1}}^{A+B\kappa}).

If
S_{0}=\emptyset
, then
G
is unramified at all finite places so
\eta^{\psi_{\infty}^{\Delta}}_{\pi_{0}}
necessarily factors through
\mathcal{S}_{\Delta}
because of the conditions on the
\chi_{G_{v}}
in Theorem
2.5.1
to glue together to a global group as in Lemma
2.1.1
.

The case when
\eta^{\psi_{\infty}^{\Delta}}_{\pi_{0}}(\mathcal{S}_{\Delta})
isn’t trivial can actually be understood more simply. If
S_{0}=\emptyset
, then each factor
f_{v}
of the test function is either on
\mathrm{GL}_{N,v}
or unramified. Therefore, its traces vanish on all
\pi_{v}\in\Pi_{\psi_{v}}
such that
\eta^{\psi_{v}}_{\pi_{v}}\neq 1
. By the multiplicity formula
2.5.4
, this implies that

m^{\psi}_{\pi}\tr_{\pi^{\infty}}(f^{\infty})=0

whenever
\eta_{\pi_{\infty}}^{\psi_{\infty}}\neq 1
. In total,

\eta^{\psi_{\infty}^{\Delta}}_{\pi_{0}}(\mathcal{S}_{\Delta})\neq 1\implies m^{G}(\pi_{0},\Delta,f^{\infty})=0.

An extension of this computation to all odd GSK shapes gives:

Theorem 10.4.1
.

Let
G
be an extended pure inner form of
G^{*}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{ell}}(N)
that is unramified at all finite places and let
\pi_{0}
be a cohomological representation of
G_{\infty}
. Choose odd GSK
\Delta=(T_{i},d_{i},\lambda_{i},\eta_{i})_{1\leq i\leq k}\in\Delta(\pi_{0})
and let
f^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}}=f_{S_{1}}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{K^{G,S}(\mathfrak{n})}
be as in Section
9.1
with
S_{0}=\emptyset
.

Then, if
\eta^{\psi_{\infty}^{\Delta}}_{\pi_{0}}(\mathcal{S}_{\Delta})\neq 1

m^{G}(\pi_{0},\Delta,f^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}})=0.

Otherwise, there are
A,B,C,D,E
with
C\geq 1
such that as long as
|\mathfrak{n}|\geq Dq_{S_{1}}^{E\kappa}
:

|\mathfrak{n}|^{R(\Delta)}\Gamma_{L(\Delta)}(\mathfrak{n})^{-1}m^{G}(\pi_{0},\Delta,f^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}})\\
=\frac{\dim\lambda_{1}}{|\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(\lambda_{1})|}\frac{\vol(H^{\prime}(F)\backslash H^{\prime}(\mathbb{A}_{f}))}{\vol(K^{S}_{H^{\prime}})\vol((H^{\prime}_{\infty})^{c})}\left(f_{S_{1}}^{H_{1}}(1)\prodf_{i>1}\int_{H_{i,\mathrm{der},S_{1}}}f^{H_{i}}_{S_{1}}(h)\,dh\right)\\
+O(|\mathfrak{n}|^{-C}q_{S_{1}}^{A+B\kappa})

where
H_{i}=H(T_{i},d_{i},\lambda_{i},\eta_{i})
,
H^{\prime}=H_{1}\times\prod_{i>1}H_{i}^{\mathrm{ab}}
, and
R(\Delta)
is as in (
9.4.1
).

For our upper bound we can allow
S_{0}\neq\emptyset
:

Theorem 10.4.2
.

Let
G
be an extended pure inner form of
G^{*}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{ell}}(N)
that may or may not be unramified and let
\pi_{0}
be a cohomological representation of
G_{\infty}
. Let
\Delta\in\Delta(\pi_{0})
and let
f^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}}=\varphi_{S_{0}}f_{S_{1}}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{K^{G,S}(\mathfrak{n})}
be as in Section
9.1
.

Then there are
A,B,D,E
such that as long as
|\mathfrak{n}|\geq Dq_{S_{1}}^{E\kappa}

m^{G}(\pi_{0},\Delta,f^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}})=O(|\mathfrak{n}|^{R(\Delta)}q_{S_{1}}^{A+B\kappa})

where
R(\Delta)
is as in Corollary
9.4.4
.

Proof.

Apply Proposition
10.2.2
and then
9.4.4
to each term.
∎

Note that Theorem
10.4.2
does
not
depend on Conjecture
6.4.1
since Corollary
9.4.4
doesn’t.

11.
Explicit Computations on Unitary Groups

In this section, we recall the explicit combinatorial parameterization of cohomological representations of
U(p,q)
and their
A
-packets and Adams-Johnson parameters following
[
MR19
]
; see also
[
VZ84
,
Tra01
,
BC05
]
. This allows us to compute the sets
\Delta(\pi_{0})
and work out explicit limit multiplicity statements from Proposition
10.2.1
together with the bounds in Theorems
10.4.1
and
10.4.2
.

11.1.
Cohomological Representations of
U(p,q)

11.1.1.
Setup:

We recall some general facts about the construction of cohomological representations.
Let
G
be a reductive Lie group with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{0}
, Cartan
\mathfrak{t}_{0}
, and
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}_{0}\otimes\mathbb{C}
. Let
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{k}\oplus\mathfrak{p}
be the Cartan decomposition corresponding to a choice of maximal compact subgroup
K
with Cartan involution
\iota
5
5

5

In lieu of the traditional
\theta
, which we reserve for the involution defining our unitary group.
. Assume that
G
has a compact Cartan subgroup
T
with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{t}\subset\mathfrak{k}
. Let
\Delta(\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{g})
be the root system for
\mathfrak{t}
in
\mathfrak{g}
.

In
[
VZ84
]
, Vogan-Zuckerman introduce the notion of a
\iota
-stable parabolic subalgebras
\mathfrak{q}
of
\mathfrak{g}
, henceforth referred to as VZ subalgebras. To construct such
\mathfrak{q}
, choose
x\in i\mathfrak{t}_{0}
and define
\lambda_{0}\in\mathfrak{t}^{*}
by
[x,\mathfrak{g}^{\alpha}]=\langle\lambda_{0},\alpha\rangle\mathfrak{g}^{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in\Delta(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{t})
. Then there are standard Levi and root space decompositions:

(11.1.1)

\mathfrak{q}:=\mathfrak{l}\oplus\mathfrak{u},\quad\mathfrak{l}:=\mathfrak{t}\oplus\bigoplus_{\langle\lambda_{0},\alpha\rangle=0}\mathfrak{g}^{\alpha},\quad\mathfrak{u}:=\oplus\bigoplus_{\langle\lambda_{0},\alpha\rangle\geq 0}\mathfrak{g}^{\alpha}.

Let
L=Z_{G}(\lambda_{0})
be the Levi subgroup of
G
with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{l}
.
Let
\Delta(\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{u})
be the roots of
\mathfrak{t}
in
\mathfrak{u}
, and let
\xi:\mathfrak{l}\to\mathbb{C}
be a character such that:

(i)

\xi
is the differential of a one-dimensional representation of
L
, and

(ii)

\langle\alpha,\xi\rangle\geq 0
for
\alpha\in\Delta(\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{u})
.

Then Vogan-Zuckerman define a representation
A_{\mathfrak{q}}(\xi)
and show that if
V_{\xi}
is the irreducible finite-dimensional
G
-representation of highest weight
\xi
, then
A_{\mathfrak{q}}(\xi)
and
V_{\xi}
have the same infinitesimal character
\xi+\rho_{G}
and

H^{*}(\mathfrak{g},K;A_{\mathfrak{q}}(\xi)\otimes V_{\xi}^{\vee})\neq 0.

Moreover, any representation with nontrivial
(\mathfrak{g},K)
-cohomology is isomorphic to
A_{\mathfrak{q}}(\xi)
for some pair
(\mathfrak{q},\xi).

11.1.2.
The parameterization

The parameterization of cohomological representations of
U(p,q)
will be given in terms of the following combinatorial data:

Definition 11.1.1
.

For a pair of non-negative integers
p,q
with
p+q=N
, let:

-

\mathcal{P}(N)
to be the set of
ordered
partitions of
N
, i.e. tuples
(N_{1},\dotsc,N_{r})
where
r
is arbitrary, each
N_{i}
is positive, and
\sum_{i}N_{i}=N
.

-

\mathcal{P}(p,q)
be the set of ordered bipartitions of
(p,q)
, i.e. the set of tuples of pairs
((p_{1},q_{1}),\dotsc,(p_{r},q_{r}))
where the
p_{i},q_{i}
are a non-negative integers with each
p_{i}+q_{i}>0
and
\sum_{i}p_{i}=p
,
\sum_{i}q_{i}=q
.

-

\mathcal{P}_{1}(p,q)\subset\mathcal{P}(p,q)
be the subset consisting of expressions
((p_{1},q_{1}),\dotsc,(p_{r},q_{r}))
where for each
i
, if either of
p_{i}
or
q_{i}=0
, then the other is
1
.

There are natural surjective maps:

(11.1.2)

\displaystyle\beta

\displaystyle:\mathcal{P}(p,q)\to\mathcal{P}(N),\quad((p_{1},q_{1}),\dotsc,(p_{r},q_{r}))\mapsto(p_{1}+q_{1},\dotsc,p_{r}+q_{r}),

(11.1.3)

\displaystyle\gamma

\displaystyle:\mathcal{P}(p,q)\to\mathcal{P}_{1}(p,q)

where
\gamma
replaces any term of the form
(n,0)
(resp.
(0,m)
) by
n
copies of
(1,0)
(resp.
m
copies of
(0,1)
.)

The bipartitions
B\in\mathcal{P}(p,q)
parameterize VZ subalgebras following
[
Tra01
, §1-3]
, which proves:

Proposition 11.1.2
.

Let
G=U(p,q)
.

(i)

There is a bijection
B\mapsto\mathfrak{q}_{B}
between
K
-conjugacy classes of VZ subalgebras of
\mathfrak{g}
and
\mathcal{P}(p,q)
.

(ii)

A_{\mathfrak{q}_{B}}(0)\simeq A_{\mathfrak{q}_{B^{\prime}}}(0)
if and only if
\gamma(B)=\gamma(B^{\prime})
.

We write the Levi subgroup
L_{B}
associated to
B\in\mathcal{P}(p,q)
as

L_{B}=U(p_{1},q_{1})\times\cdots\times U(p_{r},q_{r}).

To realize the bijection, embed
K=U(p,0)\times U(0,q)
in
U(p,q)
, write

\mathfrak{t}_{0}=\mathfrak{t}_{0}\cap\mathfrak{u}_{0}(p,0)\oplus\mathfrak{t}_{0}\cap\mathfrak{u}_{0}(0,q)\simeq\mathbb{R}^{p}\times\mathbb{R}^{q},

and assign to
B=((p_{1},q_{1}),\dotsc,(p_{r},q_{r}))
the algebra constructed from
ix_{B}
, for

x_{B}=(\overbrace{r,\dotsc,r}^{p_{1}},\overbrace{r-1,\dotsc,r-1}^{p_{2}},\dotsc,\overbrace{1,\dotsc,1}^{p_{r}},\overbrace{r,\dotsc,r}^{q_{1}},\dotsc,\overbrace{1,\dotsc,1}^{q_{r}})\in\mathfrak{t}_{0}\subset\mathfrak{k}_{0}.

Next, let
\lambda
be an infinitesimal character. Recall the notion of
P
-parts from §
1.6.3
.

Definition 11.1.3
.

We say that a regular integral infinitesimal character

\lambda=\lambda_{1}>\cdots>\lambda_{n}

is adapted to partition
P
if the
P
-parts of
\lambda
are all of the form

X^{r}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X^{(n-2i+1)/2}

for some integer or half-integer
r
and some integer
n
.

For example, the infinitesimal character
\rho_{G}
of the trivial representation is adapted to all partitions of
N
. The following is also deduced from
[
Tra01
]
:

Proposition 11.1.4
.

Let
G=U(p,q)
. The cohomological representations with regular integral infinitesimal character
\lambda
are all of the form
A_{\mathfrak{q}_{B}}(\lambda-\rho_{G})
for
B\in\mathcal{P}(p,q)
such that
\lambda
is adapted to
\beta(B)
. In particular, they are in bijection with the bipartitions
B\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(p,q)
with
\lambda
adapted to
\beta(B)
.

To compute the cohomology associated to the representations, one makes a choice of complex structure on
\mathfrak{p}
i.e. on the quotient
G/K
. To do this, fix a Shimura datum for
G
to be the conjugacy class of

h_{K}:\mathbb{S}\to U(p,q),\quad h_{K}(z)=\left(\frac{z}{\bar{z}}I_{p},I_{q}\right)\in U(p,0)\times U(0,q)\subset U(p,q).

This induces a decomposition
\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{p}^{+}\oplus\mathfrak{p}^{-}
where
\Ad(h(z))
acts on
\mathfrak{p}^{+}
by
z/\bar{z}
and on
\mathfrak{p}^{-}
by its inverse.

Lemma 11.1.5
.

Let
G=U(p,q)
with a choice of Shimura datum as above. Let
V_{\xi}
be a finite-dimensional representation with highest weight
\xi
. Let
B=((p_{1},q_{1}),\dotsc,(p_{r},q_{r}))\in\mathcal{P}(p,q)
be such that
\xi+\rho_{G}
is adapted to
\beta(B)
, and let

R=pq-\sum_{i}p_{i}q_{i},\quad R^{+}=\sum_{i<j}p_{i}q_{j},\quad R^{-}=\sum_{i>j}p_{i}q_{j}.

Then:

(i)

the smallest value of
i
such that
H^{i}(\mathfrak{g},K;A_{\mathfrak{q}_{B}}(\xi)\otimes V_{\xi})\neq 0
is
i=R
;

(ii)

H^{j}(\mathfrak{g},K;A_{\mathfrak{q}_{B}}(\xi)\otimes V_{\xi})\neq 0
if and only if
j=R+2p
for
0\leq p\leq\sum_{i}p_{i}q_{i}
;

(iii)

the Hodge weights of
A_{\mathfrak{q}_{B}}(\xi)
in degree
R+2p
are
(R^{+}+p,R^{-}+p)
.

Proof.

This follows from
[
VZ84
, Thm 3.3, 5.4, 6.19]
: the first nonzero degree of cohomology of
A_{\mathfrak{q}_{B}}(\xi)
is
R=\dim\mathfrak{u}\cap\mathfrak{p}=\dim\mathfrak{p}-\dim\mathfrak{l}\cap\mathfrak{p}=pq-\sum_{i}p_{i}q_{i}
. More precisely, the cohomology of
A_{\mathfrak{q}_{B}}(\xi)
in degree
\dim\mathfrak{u}\cap\mathfrak{p}
appears in weight
(R^{+},R^{-})=(\mathfrak{u}\cap\mathfrak{p}^{+},\mathfrak{u}\cap\mathfrak{p}^{-})
. All other weights
(a,b)
for which
A_{\mathfrak{q}}(\xi)
has cohomology are of the form
(R^{+}+p,R^{-}+p)
. One computes
R^{+}
and
R^{-}
from
x_{B}
and the choice of Shimura datum; see e.g.
[
BC05
, §5]
for more details.
∎

11.1.3.
Packets of cohomological representations

Recall form §
3.2
that any parameter
\psi
associated to a packet of cohomological representations is an AJ parameter. In particular, there is a Levi subgroup

\widehat{L}\simeq GL_{n_{1}}\times\cdots\times GL_{n_{r}}\subset\widehat{G}

such that
\psi(W_{\mathbb{C}}\times I)\subset Z(\widehat{L})
, and such that
\psi(1\times SL_{2})
is a principal
\mathrm{SL}_{2}
in
\widehat{L}
. Thus, for some
t_{i}
, we have

\psi\mid_{W_{\mathbb{C}}\times\mathrm{SL}_{2}}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}\chi_{t_{i}}\boxtimes[n_{i}],

where
[n_{i}]
is the irreducible
n_{i}
-dimensional representation of
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
and

\chi_{t_{i}}(z)=\left(\frac{z}{\bar{z}}\right)^{t_{i}/2},\quad z\in W_{\mathbb{C}}\simeq\mathbb{C}^{\times}.

In this case, the total infinitesimal character of
\psi
is

\lambda_{\psi}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}X^{\frac{t_{i}}{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{i}}X^{\frac{n_{i}-2j+1}{2}}.

Since we assume that
\lambda_{\psi}
is the infinitesimal character of a finite-dimensional representation, we have for each
i
that
t_{i}-n_{i}\equiv N\mod 2
, i.e. we are in the good parity case in the sense of
[
MR19
, §4]
. To a good parity parameter, Mœglin-Renard attach the ordered partition
P=(n_{1},\dotsc,n_{r})\in\mathcal{P}(N)
: specifically, the unordered multiset of
n_{i}
come from restricting to the Arthur-
\mathrm{SL}_{2}
and the ordering is such that the infinitesimal character of the summand
\chi_{t_{i}}\otimes[n_{i}]
of
\psi
is the
i
th
P
-part of
\lambda_{\psi}
(in particular,
P
is adapted to
\lambda_{\psi}
). They also show that the corresponding packet is

\Pi_{\psi}=\{A_{\mathfrak{q}_{B}}(\xi_{B})\mid\beta(B)=P\},

where
\xi_{B}=\boxtimes_{i}\det^{(t_{i}+n_{i}-N)/2-n_{<i}}
for
n_{<i}=\sum_{j<i}n_{j}.
In short, the representations in the packet
\pi_{\psi}
are in bijection with the bipartitions
((p_{1},q_{1}),\dotsc,(p_{r},q_{r}))\in\mathcal{P}(p,q)
such that
p_{i}+q_{i}=n_{i}
.

Mœglin-Renard do more: first they fix a Whittaker datum for a choice of quasisplit form
G^{*}
of
G
(see
[
MR19
, Rmq 4.5]
) which without loss of generality we can have match the one from Section
10.3
. Then, they write down explicitly the character
\eta_{\pi}:S^{\natural}_{\psi}\to\pm 1
attached by Arthur to each representation in the packet
\Pi_{\psi}
in terms of the bipartition
B
such that
\pi=\pi_{B}
. These constructions are recalled in §
11.3
, where they are used.

11.2.
\Delta(\pi_{0})
and
R(\Delta)

Fix an extended pure inner form
G
of
G^{*}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{sim}}(N)
. Let

\pi_{0}=\bigotimes_{v\in\infty}\pi_{0,v}

be a cohomological representation of

G_{\infty}=\prod_{v\in\infty}U(p_{v},q_{v}).

We will study the set
\Delta(\pi_{0})
introduced in §
10.1
and for each
\Delta\in\Delta(\pi_{0})
, we compute the invariant
R(\Delta)
from Theorem
9.4.4
and the set of shapes that realize it.

Definition 11.2.1
.

Let
\Delta^{\max}(\pi_{0})
be the set of
\Delta\in\Delta(\pi_{0})
with maximal
R(\Delta)
. We will denote this common maximal value
R(\pi_{0})
.

11.2.1.
Ignoring
\eta_{i}

First, since each
\Delta=(T_{i},d_{i},\lambda_{i},\eta_{i})_{i}\in\Delta(\pi_{0})
satisfies
H(\Delta)=G^{*}
, the
\eta_{i}
are determined by
T_{i}
and
d_{i}
according to Section
2.2.3
. As such, we will ignore them in this section.

11.2.2.
Arthur-
\mathrm{SL}_{2}
’s

We first study the
\Delta
according to their Arthur-
\mathrm{SL}_{2}
. These
N
-dimensional representations of
\mathrm{SL}_{2}
correspond to unordered partitions of
N
via their decomposition into irreducibles. One first sees that:

Lemma 11.2.2
.

Among the shapes
\Delta
with Arthur-
\mathrm{SL}_{2}
given by unordered partition

Q=(a_{1}^{(r_{1})},\dotsc,a_{k}^{(r_{k})})

with
a_{i}
distinct, the value of the invariant
R(\Delta)
introduced in Theorem
9.4.4
is maximized for shapes
\Delta=(r_{i},a_{i},\lambda_{i},\eta_{i})_{1\leq i\leq k}
, in which each distinct integer
a_{j}
appears once.
Denote by
R(Q)
this maximized value of
R(\Delta)
.

Furthermore, for cohomological representations
\pi_{0}
, if there is
\Delta\in\Delta(\pi_{0})
whose Arthur-
\mathrm{SL}_{2}
matches
Q
, then there is also
\Delta^{\prime}\in\Delta(\pi_{0})
such that
R(\Delta^{\prime})=R(Q)
.

Proof.

Constructing any other shape with the same Arthur-
\mathrm{SL}_{2}
would require splitting up some
(r_{i},a_{i},\lambda_{i},\eta_{i})
into smaller blocks, which would decrease
R(\Delta)
.

For the second part, we may always merge blocks in
\Delta
with the same
a_{i}
by concatenating their infinitesimal characters. This leaves
\psi^{\infty}_{\Delta}
unchanged by the construction in Lemma
4.3.4
.
∎

This reduces the study of
R(\pi_{0})
to understanding the Arthur-
\mathrm{SL}_{2}
’s for
\Delta\in\Delta(\pi_{0})
:

Definition 11.2.3
.

Let
Q^{\max}(\pi_{0})
be the set of unordered partitions representing the Arthur-
\mathrm{SL}_{2}
’s of
\Delta\in\Delta(\pi_{0})
with maximal
R(\Delta)
. Equivalently by Lemma
11.2.2
, it is the set of Arthur-
\mathrm{SL}_{2}
’s of
\Delta\in\Delta^{\max}(\pi_{0})
.

By Lemma
4.3.4
, the possible Arthur-
\mathrm{SL}_{2}
’s for
\Delta\in\Delta(\pi_{0})
are the possible Arthur-
\mathrm{SL}_{2}
’s for
\psi_{\infty}
with
\pi_{0}\in\Pi_{\psi_{\infty}}
. We can therefore enumerate them by our classification of cohomological representations.

Fix a place
v
and let
\pi_{0,v}
correspond to
B_{v}=(p_{i,v},q_{i,v})_{i}\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(p_{v},q_{v})
and infinitesimal character
\lambda_{v}
. We next study
\Delta(\pi_{0,v})
: the union of
\Delta(\pi^{\prime})
over all
\pi^{\prime}
with
\pi^{\prime}_{v}=\pi_{0,v}
. Recall from (
11.1.2
) that
\beta(B_{v})
is the ordered partition of
N
associated to
B_{v}
. We define some combinatorial objects:

•

\beta_{>1}(B)
is the unordered subpartition of
\beta(B)
corresponding to parts with size bigger than
1
.

•

Q_{p}(\pi_{0})
(resp.
Q_{q}(\pi_{0})
) is the unordered partition
(n_{j})_{j\in J}
where the
j
correspond to runs of length
n_{j}
of consecutive
(p_{i},q_{i})
of the form
(1,0)
(resp.
(0,1)
) such that the corresponding piece of
\lambda
is of the form

X^{r}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}}X^{(n-2i+1)/2}.

Next, for two unordered partitions
Q_{1}=(n_{i})_{i\in I}
and
Q_{2}=(m_{j})_{j\in J}
, we say that
Q_{2}
refines
Q_{1}
if there is a map
J\to I
such that the sum of
m_{j}
over the fiber at
i
is
n_{i}
.

Lemma 11.2.4
.

The possible Arthur-
\mathrm{SL}_{2}
’s for
\Delta\in\Delta(\pi_{0,v})
correspond exactly to unordered partitions

(X,Y,\beta_{>1}(B_{v})),

where
X
refines
Q_{p}(\pi_{0,v})
and
Y
refines
Q_{q}(\pi_{0,v})
.

Proof.

Lemma
11.1.5
tells us that the
P\in\mathcal{P}(p+q)
that correspond to
\psi_{\infty}
with
\pi_{0}\in\Pi_{\psi_{\infty}}
are produced by merging runs of consecutive
1
’s in
\beta(B_{v})
that correspond to parts in
B_{v}
all of the form
(1,0)
or all of the form
(0,1)
. Furthermore, we require that the coarsened partition thereby produced is still adapted to
\lambda_{v}
. These conditions together show that
P
is an ordering of something of the form
(X,Y,\beta_{>1}(B_{v}))
.

Consider such a partition

P=(n_{1},\dotsc,n_{k}).

We will show that there is
\Delta\in\Delta(\pi_{0,v})
with
\psi^{\Delta}_{v}
corresponding to
P
. Let
I_{1}
be the subset of indices such that
n_{i}=1
and
I_{+}
its complement. Let
\lambda_{v}
have
P
-parts
\lambda^{P}_{1},\dotsc,\lambda^{P}_{k}
.
Since
\lambda_{v}
is adapted to
P
, for each
i\in I_{+}
, there exists
\lambda_{i,v}
such that the shape
(1,n_{i},\lambda_{i,v})
has total infinitesimal character
\lambda^{P}_{i}
at
v
. Next, let
\lambda^{\prime}_{v}
be the concatenation of
\lambda^{P}_{i}
for
i\in I_{1}
. Finally, choose the other components for
w\neq v
of
\lambda_{i}
and
\lambda^{\prime}
arbitrarily. Consider

\Delta=(|I_{1}|,1,\lambda^{\prime},\eta),((1,n_{i},\lambda_{i},\eta_{i}))_{i\in I_{+}}.

Then, by the constructions in §
11.1.3
,
\psi^{\Delta}_{v}
corresponds to
P
. Note that
\Delta\in\Delta(\pi_{0,v})
by the form of
P
.
∎

Next, we define

\beta_{>1}(\pi_{0}):=\bigcup_{v\in\infty}\beta_{>1}(B_{v})

where the union is interpreted as of non-disjoint multisets (the multiplicity of an element is exactly equal to the maximum of its multiplicities in the
\beta_{>1}(B_{v})
).

Lemma 11.2.5
.

The possible Arthur-
\mathrm{SL}_{2}
’s for
\Delta\in\Delta(\pi_{0})
correspond exactly to unordered partitions
Q
such that, for each
v\in\infty
,
Q
can be written in the form:

Q=(X_{v},Y_{v},\beta_{>1}(B_{v}))

where
X_{v}
refines
Q_{p}(\pi_{0,v})
and
Y_{v}
refines
Q_{q}(\pi_{0,v})
. In particular:

•

All Arthur-
\mathrm{SL}_{2}
’s for
\Delta\in\Delta(\pi_{0})
contain
\beta_{>1}(\pi_{0})
as a subpartition,

•

if
\Delta(\pi_{0})
isn’t empty, there is
\Delta\in\Delta(\pi_{0})
with Arthur-
\mathrm{SL}_{2}

(1,\dotsc,1,\beta_{>1}(\pi_{0})).

Proof.

That the Arthur-
\mathrm{SL}_{2}
’s must be contained in this set is an elementary combinatorial extension of the argument in
11.2.4
.

Existence of a
\Delta=(T_{i},d_{i},\lambda_{i})_{i}
with a particular
\mathrm{SL}_{2}
follows from fixing each component of the
\lambda_{i}
as in the argument of Lemma
11.2.4
instead of just the
v
-component.

Finally, the two bullet points are also elementary combinatorial properties of the set of such simultaneous
(X_{v},Y_{v},\beta_{>1}(B_{v}))
.
∎

Note
.

We summarize this section as a three step algorithm for computing
\Delta^{\max}(\pi_{0})
:

(1)

Find the possible Arthur-
\mathrm{SL}_{2}
’s for
\Delta\in\Delta(\pi_{0})
by Lemma
11.2.5
.

(2)

Compute
R(Q)
for each of these Arthur-
\mathrm{SL}_{2}
’s to compute
Q^{\max}(\pi_{0})
.

(3)

\Delta^{\max}(\pi_{0})
is then partitioned into non-empty parts corresponding to the
Q\in Q^{\max}(\pi_{0})
. Each part can be determined by Lemma
11.2.2
.

It turns out that the second step becomes much easier for GSK-shapes. Showing this will make up the remainder of our combinatorial work.

11.2.3.
The key bound

We next prove an elementary combinatorial bound that is basically
a reformulation of Lemma 7.1 in
[
MS19
]
. Recall the numerical invariants
\bar{R}(\Delta)
and
R(\Delta)
, introduced respectively in (
9.3.4
) and (
9.4.1
);

R(Q)
is the maximum of
R(\Delta)
over the
\Delta
such that
\psi_{\infty}^{\Delta}
corresponds to
Q
.
Part of the complexity of this argument is an artifact of only being able to prove the suboptimal bound
R(\Delta)
from Corollary
9.4.4
instead of the optimal
R_{0}(\Delta)
from Conjecture
9.6.3
.

Lemma 11.2.6
.

Let
Q_{0}
be an unordered partition that has distinct parts and no parts of size
1
. Then the maximum value of
R(Q)
over all unordered partitions
Q
of
N
that have subpartition
Q_{0}
is achieved by

Q_{\mathrm{can}}=(1^{(r)},Q_{0}).

Furthermore, if either
r\neq 2
or
Q_{0}
has no parts of size
2
, this is the unique such
Q
that achieves this maximum.

Proof.

Let

Q_{\mathrm{can}}=(1,\dotsc,1,Q_{0})=(1^{(r)},(a_{i}^{(r_{i})})_{i})

for
a_{i}
distinct and
r_{i}=1
. The other possible
Q
containing
Q_{0}
are produced by decreasing the number of
1
’s and increasing one of the
r_{i}
’s. We will therefore show that
R(Q)
decreases if we increase any of the
r_{i}
.

Recall from Remark
Remark
that
R(Q)
is obtained by starting from
\bar{R}(Q)
(which is equal to the dimension of a certain parabolic block matrix) and then replacing the dimensions of certain blocks on the diagonal with modified counts. Along the diagonal, the blocks are indexed by the number
1
and the distinct
a_{i}
. Changing an
r_{i}
changes three blocks of the parabolic: the two on the diagonal associated to
1
and
a_{i}
and one corresponding to this pair above the diagonal. In particular, we can treat changes in each
r_{i}
independently. Furthermore, changing an
r_{i}
from
0
(i.e. creating a new block) can be easily seen to decrease
R(Q)
.

In general, going from
r_{i}=1
to
r_{i}=k
changes the modified summand associated to these three blocks from:

r^{2}+ra_{i}+1\mapsto\\
(r-(k-1)a_{i})^{2}+ka_{i}(r-(k-1)a_{i})+(\text{modified count for $a_{i}$ with $r_{i}=k$}).

Expanding out, the change in
R(Q)
is

(\text{modified count for $a_{i}$ with $r_{i}=k$})-a_{i}(a_{i}+r)(k-1)-1,

where the modified counts are the counts for the part of formula for
R(Q)
associated to the
a_{i}
-block on the diagonal. The modified count defining
R(Q)
is defined differently for
r_{i}=1,2,3
versus everything else. Therefore we look at cases:

•

If an
r_{i}
is increased to
2
, the modified count is

2a_{i}(a_{i}-1)+(a_{i}+3)
,
making the total difference equal to

2a_{i}(a_{i}-1)+(a_{i}+3)-a_{i}(a_{i}+r)-1.

Using
r\geq a_{i}
, this is at most
-a_{i}+2

and is always negative unless
r=a_{i}=2
.

•

If an
r_{i}
is increased to
3
, the modified count is

\frac{9}{2}a_{i}(a_{i}-1)+((4+\epsilon)a_{i}+5)
,
making the total difference

\frac{9}{2}a_{i}(a_{i}-1)+((4+\epsilon)a_{i}+5)-2a_{i}(a_{i}+r)-1.

Using
r\geq 2a_{i}
, this is bounded above by

-\frac{3}{2}a_{i}^{2}-\left(\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon\right)a_{i}+4,

which is always negative for in particular
\epsilon<1/2
since
a_{i}\geq 2
.

•

If an
r_{i}
is increased to
k\geq 4
, then the change in modified counts is
\frac{k^{2}}{2}a_{i}(a_{i}+1)
,
making the total difference

\frac{k^{2}}{2}a_{i}(a_{i}+1)-a_{i}(a_{i}+r)(k-1)-1.

Using
r\geq(k-1)a_{i}
, this is bounded above by

-\left(\frac{k^{2}}{2}-k\right)a_{i}^{2}+\frac{k^{2}}{2}a_{i}-1

and using
a_{i}\geq 2
and
k^{2}/2-k>0
gives an upper bound by

-\left(\frac{k^{2}}{2}-2k\right)a_{i}-1,

which is always negative when
k\geq 4
.

In total, if we increase any of the
r_{i}
, then
R(Q)
decreases.
∎

11.2.4.
Summary of combinatorial work

To conclude:

Definition 11.2.7
.

Let
\pi_{0}=\bigotimes_{v\mid\infty}\pi_{0,v}
, with
\pi_{0,v}
from
B_{v}=(p_{i,v},q_{i,v})_{v}\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(p_{v},q_{v})
. Let
\beta_{>1}(B_{v})
be the unordered subpartiton of pieces of size greater than
1
in
\beta(B_{v})
. Let
\beta_{>1}(\pi_{0})
be the union of all the
\beta_{>1}(B_{v})
as multisets. Finally, define

Q_{\mathrm{can}}(\pi_{0}):=(1,\dotsc,1,\beta_{>1}(\pi_{0}))

as an unordered partition of
N
(if it exists).

Then:

Proposition 11.2.8
.

Let
Q_{\mathrm{can}}(\pi_{0})
be of the form

Q_{\mathrm{can}}(\pi_{0})=(1^{(r)},a_{1},\dotsc,a_{k})

for the
a_{i}
distinct (i.e.
\beta_{>1}(\pi_{0})
has distinct parts). Further assume that either
r\neq 2
or there is no
i
such that
a_{i}=2
. Then
\Delta^{\max}(\pi_{0})
consists of all shapes of the form

(r,1,\lambda),(1,a_{i},\lambda_{i})_{1\leq i\leq k}

in
\Delta(\pi_{0})
and
\Delta^{\max}(\pi_{0})\neq\emptyset
provided that
\Delta(\pi_{0})\neq\emptyset
.

Proof.

This is the result of applying algorithm
Note
keeping Lemma
11.2.6
in mind for step (2) to get that
Q^{\max}(\pi_{0})=\{Q_{\mathrm{can}}(\pi_{0})\}
.
∎

We warn that Proposition
11.2.8
does not neccesarily hold if
\beta_{>1}(\pi_{0})
has repeated elements. For example, consider
\beta_{>1}(\pi_{0})=(2,2,2,2)
. Then we have

R(2^{(4)},1,1)=67<R(2^{(5)})=74.

In fact, this is a counterexample to even the analogous statement with the conjectural optimal bound
R_{0}
. Whether any
\Delta\in\Delta(\pi_{0})
can have Arthur-
\mathrm{SL}_{2}
given by
(2^{(5)})
depends on what exactly the
Q_{p}(\pi_{0,v})
and
Q_{q}(\pi_{0,v})
are. Therefore, a general description of
Q^{\max}(\pi_{0})
is much more complicated.

11.2.5.
GSK-maxed representations

Now we restrict to the special class of representations we can study:

Definition 11.2.9
.

Let
\pi_{0}
be a cohomological representation of some
U(p,q)
corresponding to bipartition
(p_{i},q_{i})_{i}\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(p+q)
. We say
\pi_{0}
is GSK-maxed if the only value of
p_{i}+q_{i}
that appears with multiplicity is
1
.

We say
\pi_{0}
is odd GSK-maxed if in addition all the
p_{i}+q_{i}
are odd.

Definition 11.2.10
.

Let
\pi_{0}
factor into
\pi_{0,v}
that each corresponding to bipartition
(p_{i,v},q_{i,v})
. Then we say that
\pi_{0}
is GSK-maxed if
\Delta(\pi_{0})\neq\emptyset
and the only number that appears with multiplicity among the
p_{i,v}+q_{i,v}
is
1
.

We say
\pi_{0}
is odd GSK-maxed if in addition all the
p_{i,v}+q_{i,v}
are odd. Equivalently,
\beta_{>1}(\pi_{0})
is a partition into (odd) distinct parts.

These definitions are justified by the following corollary of Proposition
11.2.8
:

Corollary 11.2.11
.

Fix an extended pure inner form
G
of
G^{*}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{ell}}(N)
and let
\pi_{0}
be a cohomological representation of
G_{\infty}
that is (odd) GSK-maxed. Further assume that if
\beta(\pi_{0})=(1^{(r)},a_{1},\dotsc,a_{k})
, then either
r\neq 2
or none of the
a_{i}=2
(this is automatically satisfied if
\pi_{0}
is odd GSK-maxed).

Then all elements of
\Delta^{\max}(\pi_{0})
are (odd) GSK.

We warn that in general,
\Delta^{\max}(\pi_{0})
isn’t a singleton. The different possibilities differ by different assignments of infinitesimal characters
\lambda_{i,v}
to each block
(T_{i},d_{i})
.

Example
.

Consider
F
with two infinite places
v,w
and
G_{v}\cong G_{w}\cong U(6,1)
. Let

\pi_{0,v}=(1,1),(1,0)^{(5)},\quad\text{and}\quad\pi_{0,w}=(2,1),(1,0)^{(4)}

have the infinitesimal character of the trivial representation:

\lambda=(3,2,1,0,-1,-2,-3).

If
\Delta^{\max}(\pi_{0})\neq\emptyset
, any
\Delta\in\Delta^{\max}(\pi_{0})
is of the form

\Delta=(2,1,(\lambda^{1}_{v},\lambda^{1}_{w})),(1,2,(\lambda^{2}_{v},\lambda^{2}_{w})),(1,3,(\lambda^{3}_{v},\lambda^{3}_{w})),

and the unordered partition
Q^{\max}(\pi_{0})
is
(3,2,1,1)
.
We are forced to choose

\lambda^{2}_{v}[2]=(3,2),\qquad\lambda^{3}_{w}[3]=(3,2,1).

However, we still need to pick
\lambda^{3}_{v}
and
\lambda^{2}_{w}
. This will correspond to a choice of ordering of
Q^{\max}(\pi_{0})
at each of
v
and
w
.

There are three choices

\lambda^{3}_{v}[3]=(1,0,-1),(0,-1,-2),\text{ or }(-1,-2,-3)

corresponding to three choices

\lambda^{1}_{v}=(-2,-3),(1,-3),\text{ or }(1,0)

and three orderings of
Q^{\max}(\pi_{0})
:

(2,3,1,1),(2,1,3,1),\text{ or }(2,1,1,3).

Similarly, there are three choices

\lambda^{2}_{w}[2]=(0,-1),(-1,-2),\text{ or }(-2,-3)

corresponding to three choices

\lambda^{1}_{w}=(-2,-3),(0,-3),\text{ or }(0,-1)

and three orderings of
Q^{\max}(\pi_{0})
:

(3,2,1,1),(3,1,2,1),\text{ or }(3,1,1,2).

Thus in total
\Delta^{\max}(\pi_{0})
can contain up to nine elements. Note that the different possibilities for
\lambda^{1}_{v}
(resp.
\lambda^{1}_{w}
) aren’t necessarily even character twists of each other.

Example
.

As an simple example where this difficulty doesn’t appear, assume

G_{\infty}=U(N-1,1)^{r}

and let
\pi_{0}\cong\pi_{0,v}^{r}
be diagonal. There is only one non-
1
entry in any element of
\mathcal{P}_{1}(N-1,1)
so
\pi_{0}
is GSK-maxed. The arguments in Lemmas
11.2.4
and
11.2.5
show that since
\beta_{>1}(\pi_{0})=\beta_{>1}(B_{v})
is a singleton, there is exactly one way to assign infinitesimal characters
\lambda_{i,v}
to the blocks
(T_{i},d_{i})
, so that
\Delta^{\max}(\pi_{0})
is a singleton.

11.3.
Characters of the Component Group

As a last piece of the puzzle to derive explicit limit multiplicities for individual representations from Theorem
10.4.1
, we attach to elements of the AJ-packet
\Pi_{\psi_{\infty}}
characters of the group
S^{\natural}_{\psi_{\infty}}
introduced in §
2.4.2
. Let
P=(a_{1},\dotsc,a_{k})\in\mathcal{P}(N)
correspond to an
A
-parameter
\psi_{\infty}
at infinity for
U(p,q)
considered as an extended pure inner form.
As mentioned after
[
MR19
, (1.3)]
, the equation (
3.2.1
) reduces to a canonical isomorphism

S^{\natural}_{\psi_{\infty}}=\bigoplus_{1\leq i\leq k}\mathbb{Z}/2=\langle\epsilon_{i}\rangle_{1\leq i\leq k}.

In addition, the subgroup
\mathbb{Z}(\widehat{G})^{\Gamma}
is the diagonally embedded
\mathbb{Z}/2
.

Though the character attached to a representation in a local
A
-packet is not explicit in general, Mœglin-Renard in
[
MR19
]
make it so for AJ-packets on unitary groups. Let

\pi_{0}=(p_{i},q_{i})_{i}\in\beta^{-1}(P)\subseteq\mathcal{P}(p,q)

and define
a_{<i}=\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}a_{i}
. Then,

\eta^{\psi_{\infty}}_{\pi_{0}}(\epsilon_{i})=(-1)^{p_{i}a_{<i}+q_{i}(a_{<i}+1)+a_{i}(a_{i}-1)/2}.

We can simplify this to

(11.3.1)

\eta^{\psi_{\infty}}_{\pi_{0}}(\epsilon_{i})=(-1)^{a_{i}a_{<i}+q_{i}+\chi_{4}(a_{i})},

where

\chi_{4}(a_{i}):=\begin{cases}0&a_{i}\equiv 0,1\pmod{4}\\
1&a_{i}\equiv 2,3\pmod{4}\end{cases}.

If the
a_{i}
are all odd, this further simplifies to

(11.3.2)

\eta^{\psi_{\infty}}_{\pi_{0}}(\epsilon_{i})=(-1)^{(i-1)+q_{i}+\chi_{4}(a_{i})}.

Finally, if
\psi_{\infty}=\psi_{\infty}^{\Delta}
for
\Delta
of the type in Lemma
11.2.2
, then

\mathcal{S}^{\natural}_{\Delta}=\left\langle s_{d}:=\sum_{i:a_{i}=d}\epsilon_{i}\right\rangle_{d\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}}

is a subgroup of
S^{\natural}_{\psi_{\infty}}
. As a result, we can characterize representations such that
\eta^{\psi_{\infty}^{\Delta}}_{\pi_{0}}(S^{\natural}_{\Delta})=1
; this will be used to give asymptotics for multiplicities of individual representations in the odd GSK-maxed case.

Lemma 11.3.1
.

Let
G
be an extended pure inner form of
G^{*}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{ell}}(N)
. Let
\pi_{0}=\bigotimes_{v}\pi_{0,v}
be an odd GSK-maxed representation of
G_{\infty}=\prod_{v}U(p_{v},q_{v})
.

Let
Q^{\max}(\pi_{0})=(1^{(r)},d_{1},\dotsc,d_{k})
with
d_{i}
odd and distinct. The
\Delta\in\Delta^{\max}(\pi_{0})
each determine orderings

P^{\Delta}_{v}:=(a_{1,v},\dotsc,a_{k+r,v})

of
Q^{\max}(\pi_{0})
for each
v
such that
\psi^{\Delta}_{v}=P^{\Delta}_{v}
. Let

\pi_{0,v}=(p^{\Delta}_{i,v},q^{\Delta}_{i,v})_{i}\in\beta^{-1}(P^{\Delta}_{v})\subseteq\mathcal{P}(p_{v},q_{v})

and for each
d_{j}
, let
i^{\Delta}_{v}(d_{j})
be the index
i
such that
d_{j}=a_{i,v}
.

Then, for
\Delta\in\Delta^{\max}(\pi_{0})
,
\eta^{\psi_{\infty}^{\Delta}}_{\pi_{0}}(S^{\natural}_{\Delta})=1
if and only if

t_{j}:=\sum_{v\in\infty}(i^{\Delta}_{v}(d_{j})-1+q^{\Delta}_{i^{\Delta}_{v}(d_{j}),v}+\chi_{4}(d_{j}))

is even for each
1\leq j\leq k
and
G_{\infty}
satisfies the parity conditions from
2.1.1
.

Proof.

The condition on
t_{j}
comes from checking that
\eta_{\pi_{0}}^{\psi_{\infty}^{\Delta}}(s_{d})=1
for
d>1
. The second comes from checking that
\eta_{\pi_{0}}^{\psi_{\infty}^{\Delta}}
factors through
\mathcal{S}_{\Delta}
.
∎

Beware that it is important to keep track of whether
G_{v}=U(p,q)
or
U(q,p)
as an extended pure inner form to compute the characters
\eta^{\psi_{\infty}}_{\pi_{0}}
.

11.4.
Explicit Limit Multiplicities

Now that we understand
\Delta(\pi_{0})
, we can compute our main result: exact limit multiplicities for odd GSK-maxed
\pi_{0}
.

Theorem 11.4.1
.

Let
G
be a pure inner form of
G^{*}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{ell}}(N)
. Choose

f^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}}=f_{S_{1}}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{K^{G,S}(\mathfrak{n})}

as in Section
9.1
with
S_{0}=\emptyset
(in particular: only split places divide
\mathfrak{n}
and
G
and therefore
E/F
is unramified at all finite places as in Lemma
2.1.1
).

Pick a cohomological representation
\pi_{0}
of
G_{\infty}
that is odd GSK-maxed and for

\Delta=(T_{1},1,\lambda_{1},\eta_{1}),(1,d_{i},\lambda_{i},\eta_{i})_{2\leq i\leq k}\in\Delta^{\max}(\pi_{0}),

let
\lambda_{1}(\Delta)=\lambda_{1}
. Let
T_{1}
,
d_{i}
,
R
and
L
be the common values of
T_{1}
,
d_{i}
,
R(\Delta)
and
L(\Delta)
over
\Delta^{\max}(\pi_{0})
. Then there are
A,B,C,D,E
with
C\geq 1
such that if
|\mathfrak{n}|\geq Dq_{S_{1}}^{E\kappa}
:

|\mathfrak{n}|^{-R}\Gamma_{L}(\mathfrak{n})^{-1}\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{AR}_{\mathrm{disc}}(G)}\mathbf{1}_{\pi_{\infty}=\pi_{0}}\tr_{\pi^{\infty}}(f^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}})\\
=\frac{\vol(H(F)\backslash H(\mathbb{A}_{f}))}{\vol(K^{S}_{H})}\left(\sum_{\Delta\in\Delta^{\max}(\pi_{0})}\mathbf{1}_{\eta_{\pi_{0}}^{\psi^{\Delta}_{\infty}}(\mathcal{S}_{\Delta})=1}\frac{\dim\lambda_{1}(\Delta)}{|\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(\lambda_{1}(\Delta))|}\right)\\
\times\left(f_{S_{1}}^{H_{1}}(1)\prodf_{i>1}\int_{H_{i,\mathrm{der},S_{1}}}f^{H_{i}}_{S_{1}}(h)\,dh\right)+O(|\mathfrak{n}|^{-C}q_{S_{1}}^{A+B\kappa}),

where
H_{i}=H(T_{i},d_{i},\lambda_{i},\eta_{i})
and
H=H_{1}\times\prod_{i>1}H_{i}^{\mathrm{ab}}
are both constant over
\Delta\in\Delta^{\max}(\pi_{0})
.

Finally, recall that the condition on
\eta_{\pi_{0}}^{\psi^{\Delta}_{\infty}}
can be checked as in Lemma
11.3.1
, that

R=(k-1)+\frac{1}{2}\left(N^{2}+T_{1}^{2}-\sum_{i\geq 2}d_{i}^{2},\right),

and that

L=T_{1},1^{(k-1)},-1^{(k-1)}.

Proof.

Apply Proposition
10.2.2
. Then Corollary
11.2.11
allows us to apply Theorem
10.4.1
to compute the main terms
m(\pi_{0},\Delta,f^{\infty})
for
\Delta\in\Delta^{\max}(\pi_{0})
. We compute
R=R(\pi_{0})
by Corollary
11.2.8
. Theorem
10.4.2
bounds all the terms
m(\pi_{0},\Delta^{\prime},f^{\infty})
for
\Delta^{\prime}\notin\Delta^{\max}(\pi_{0})
.
∎

Note
.

Since the different shapes
\Delta=(T_{i},d_{i},\lambda_{i},\eta_{i})_{i}\in\Delta^{\max}(\pi_{0})
only differ in their
\lambda_{i}
-coordinates, they correspond to the same map

\mathcal{S}_{\Delta}:\widehat{H}^{\mathrm{ur},\mathrm{temp}}_{v}\to\widehat{G}^{\mathrm{ur}}_{v}

Therefore, as in (
9.5.2
), we can compute

f_{S_{1}}^{H_{1}}(1)\prodf_{i>1}\int_{H_{i,\mathrm{der},S_{1}}}f^{H_{i}}_{S_{1}}(h)\,dh=\mu^{\mathrm{pl}(\pi_{0}),\mathrm{ur}}_{S_{1}}(\widehat{f}_{S_{1}}),

where
\mu^{\mathrm{pl}(\pi_{0}),\mathrm{ur}}
is the common value of the
\mu^{\pi(\Delta),\mathrm{ur}}
. This interprets Theorem
11.4.1
as an unramified “Plancherel” equidistribution theorem for the local component
\pi_{S}
as in
[
ST16
]
. Beware that
\mu^{\mathrm{pl}(\pi_{0}),\mathrm{ur}}
can have support on the non-tempered spectrum.

For general representations, we have an upper bound:

Theorem 11.4.2
.

Let
G
be an extended pure inner form of
G^{*}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{ell}}(N)
. Choose

f^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}}=\varphi_{S_{0}}f_{S_{1}}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{K^{G,S}(\mathfrak{n})}

as in §
9.1
with
f_{S_{1}}
and
\varphi_{S_{0}}
arbitrary (In particular, only split places divide
\mathfrak{n}
).

Pick a cohomological representation
\pi_{0}
and let
R_{0}(\pi_{0})
be defined as in the end of §
Note
. Then if
\Delta(\pi_{0})=\emptyset
,

\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{AR}_{\mathrm{disc}}(G)}\mathbf{1}_{\pi_{\infty}=\pi_{0}}=0.

Otherwise, there are
A,B,D,E
such that as long as
|\mathfrak{n}|\geq Dq_{S_{1}}^{E\kappa}
:

\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{AR}_{\mathrm{disc}}(G)}\mathbf{1}_{\pi_{\infty}=\pi_{0}}\tr_{\pi^{\infty}}(f^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}})=O(|\mathfrak{n}|^{R(\pi_{0})}q_{S_{1}}^{A+B\kappa}).

Proof.

Apply Proposition
10.2.2
and Theorem
10.4.2
.
∎

The above result applies to any CM extension
E/F
and any extended pure inner form
G
.
Assuming Conjecture
9.6.3
, the exponent
R(\pi_{0})
can be improved to a tighter
R_{0}(\pi_{0})
. Furthermore, in the sum from Theorem
10.2.1
, at least one of the
\Delta_{1}\times\Delta_{2}
for each
\Delta\in\Delta^{\max}(\pi_{0})
satisfies that the
d_{i}
assigned to each
\Delta_{i}
all have the same parity. Therefore, if we assume the even stronger Conjecture
9.6.4
and don’t have an obstruction from the multiplicity formula (like the
\eta^{\Delta}_{\pi_{0}}
condition from Theorem
11.4.2
), we expect
R_{0}(\pi_{0})
to be optimal.

12.
Examples and Corollaries

This section contains examples and corollaries of the main Theorems
11.4.1
and
11.4.2
, including applications to Sato-Tate equidistribution in families, the Sarnak-Xue density hypothesis, and the cohomology of locally symmetric spaces. We first recall all our various growth rates for reader’s convenience:

Let
\pi_{0}
be on a rank-
N
group. Each
\star(\pi_{0})
is a maximum of
\star(\Delta)=\star((T_{i},d_{i},\lambda_{i},\eta_{i})_{i})
for
\Delta\in\Delta(\pi_{0})
. Then there are two formulas:

First, the provable growth rate from Proposition
9.4.4
, which will be used in all theorem statements is:

(12.0.1)

R(\pi_{0})=\max_{\Delta\in\Delta(\pi_{0})}\frac{1}{2}\left(N^{2}+\sum_{i}T_{i}^{2}d_{i}\right)-\sum_{i:T_{i}=1}\left(\frac{1}{2}(d_{i}^{2}+d_{i})-1\right)-\\
\sum_{i:T_{i}=2}\left(3d_{i}-3\right)-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i:T_{i}=3\\
d_{i}>1\end{subarray}}\left((5-10^{-100})d_{i}+5\right).

It will be compared to the conjecturally sharp growth rate from
9.6.3
:

R_{0}(\pi_{0})=\max_{\Delta\in\Delta(\pi_{0})}\frac{1}{2}\left(N^{2}-\sum_{i}T_{i}^{2}d_{i}^{2}\right)+\sum_{i}\left(T_{i}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}T_{i}(T_{i}-1)(d_{i}^{2}-1)\right).

12.1.
Examples

First, we work out what Theorem
11.4.1
says in some simple cases. To discuss infinitesimal characters, let
\lambda_{1},\dotsc,\lambda_{n-1}
be the fundamental weights of
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
:

\lambda_{i}=(1^{(i)},0^{(N-i)}),

in the standard basis of
X^{*}(T)
corresponding to the entries of a diagonal matrix. Define
\lambda_{0},\lambda_{n}
similarly for indexing purposes. Two weights are character twists of each other if they differ by a multiple of
\lambda_{n}
and the half-sum of positive roots is

\rho_{n}=\left(\frac{n-1}{2},\frac{n-3}{2},\dotsc,\frac{1-n}{2}\right).

12.1.1.
Example 1: parallel case for
U(N-1,1)
.

For the simplest example with non-tempered representations at infinity, assume:

•

\deg F/\mathbb{Q}=d
is even,

•

G_{\infty}\cong U(N-1,1)^{d}
as is allowed by §
2.1
,

•

\pi_{\infty}\cong\pi_{0}^{d}
with
\pi_{0}=((1,0)^{(r)},(k-1,1),(1,0)^{(N-k-r)})
for
k>1
odd,

•

S_{1}=\emptyset
.

Then
\Delta^{\max}(\pi_{0})
is a singleton

(N-k,1,(\lambda_{1,v})_{v},\eta_{1}),(1,k,(\lambda_{2,v})_{v},\eta_{2}),

with
\lambda_{1,v}
a character twist of
k\lambda_{r}+\rho_{n-k}
on
\mathrm{GL}_{n-k}
and
\lambda_{2,v}
the infinitesimal character of a
1
-dimensional irrep. We recall that the discrete
L
-packet at infinitesimal character
\lambda_{2,v}
has size
N
.
Finally,
d
is even, so
\eta_{\pi_{\infty}}^{\psi_{\infty}}=(\eta_{\pi_{0}}^{\psi_{v}})^{d}
is trivial.

Denoting by
\pi_{k\lambda_{r}}
the finite-dimensional representation of
\mathrm{GL}_{N-k}(\mathbb{C})
with highest weight
k\lambda_{r}
, we compute

(12.1.1)

|\mathfrak{n}|^{-(N(N-k)+1)}L_{k,1,-1}(\mathfrak{n})^{-1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in\mathcal{AR}_{\mathrm{disc}}(G)\\
\pi_{\infty}=\pi_{0}\end{subarray}}\dim((\pi^{\infty})^{K(\mathfrak{n})})\\
=\frac{1}{N^{d}}\dim(\pi_{k\lambda_{r}})^{d}\tau^{\prime}(U_{E/F}(N-k)\times U(1))+O(|\mathfrak{n}|^{-C}q_{S_{1}}^{A+B\kappa})

as an asymptotic count of automorphic
forms
of level
\mathfrak{n}
corresponding to
\pi_{\infty}
. Recall that
\tau^{\prime}
is the modified Tamagawa number from (
9.1.1
).

Note that the “masses” (i.e. relative abundances in the automorphic spectrum)

\frac{1}{N^{d}}\dim(\pi_{k\lambda_{r}})^{d}

depend on
r
even though the corresponding
\pi_{0}
have the same infinitesimal character and come from the same Levi in the Langlands classification. This is because the way in which the infinitesimal character divides up between the blocks of this Levi also matters.

More specifically,
\dim(\pi_{k\lambda_{r}})
is largest for
r
close to
(N-k)/2
and decreases to
1
towards
r=0
or
N-k
. For another prespective, all representations considered are cohomological of degree
d(N-k)
. The representations with Hodge weights closer to
\left(\frac{d}{2}(N-k),\frac{d}{2}(N-k)\right)
have larger masses, whereas the ones whose weights are closer to
(0,d(N-k))
and
(d(N-k),0)
are rarer.

12.1.2.
Example 2

As a slight complication, now assume:

•

\deg F/\mathbb{Q}=d
is odd,

•

N\not\equiv 0\pmod{4}
and, to satisfy the conditions in §
2.1
:

G_{\infty}\cong\begin{cases}U(N-1,1)^{d}&N\equiv 2,3\pmod{4}\\
U(1,N-1)^{d}&N\equiv 1\pmod{4}\end{cases}

•

\pi_{\infty}=\pi_{0}^{d}
with

\pi_{0}=\begin{cases}((1,0)^{(r)},(k-1,1),(1,0)^{(N-k-r)})&N\equiv 2,3\pmod{4}\\
((0,1)^{(r)},(1,k-1),(0,1)^{(N-k-r)})&N\equiv 1\pmod{4}\end{cases}

for
k>1
odd and
r+k\leq N
,

•

S_{1}=\emptyset
.

This situation is similar to the first example, but no longer necessarily has
\eta_{\pi_{\infty}}^{\Delta}=1
.

Using the test from Lemma
11.3.1
,
\eta_{\pi_{\infty}}^{\Delta}=1
if and only if

r+\begin{cases}1&N\equiv 2,3\pmod{4}\\
N-1&N\equiv 1\pmod{4}\end{cases}+\begin{cases}1&k\equiv 0,1\pmod{4}\\
0&k\equiv 2,3\pmod{4}\end{cases}\equiv 0\pmod{2}

is even. We write this condition as

(12.1.2)

r+1+\chi_{4}(N)+\chi_{4}(k)\equiv 0\pmod{2},

with
\chi_{4}
the character from Lemma
11.3.1
and think of it as a parity condition on
r
.

If (
12.1.2
) holds, then we have the same result (
12.1.1
) as in the previous example. Otherwise, we have that

(12.1.3)

|\mathfrak{n}|^{-(N(N-k)+1)}L_{k,1,-1}(\mathfrak{n})^{-1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in\mathcal{AR}_{\mathrm{disc}}(G)\\
\pi_{\infty}=\pi_{0}\end{subarray}}\dim((\pi^{\infty})^{K(\mathfrak{n})})=O(|\mathfrak{n}|^{-C}q_{S_{1}}^{A+B\kappa}).

There are two consequences. First, we only have exact asymptotics in the case where
r
satisfies (
12.1.2
). Second, the asymptotic growth rate of counts of forms can be different for representations coming from the same Levi in the Langlands classification.

These phenomena are caused by an obstruction from the multiplicity formula in our specific setup: the automorphic representations counted have unramified local components at all non-split finite places. In particular, they all correspond to the trivial character on the component group. Furthermore, all those representations came from parameters
\psi
with
\epsilon_{\psi}=1
. In total, the multiplicity formula requires
\eta^{\psi}_{\pi_{0}}=1
for the packet
\Pi_{\psi}
to contribute to the multiplicity of
\pi_{0}
.

More surprisingly, growth rates can differ even within an
L
-packet. If
\pi_{0}=((p_{i},q_{i}))_{i}
on
U(p,q)
, it can be seen from the description in
[
VZ84
, §6]
that some other members of its (pseudo- and therefore true)
L
-packet can be produced by reversing some pairs
(p_{i},q_{i})
such that we remain in
\mathcal{P}(p,q)
. For an
L
-packet like

\{((2,1),(0,1)),((1,2),(1,0))\}

on
U(2,2)
, only one member satisfies the parity condition from Lemma
11.3.1
. This is starkly different from the discrete-at-infinity case in
[
Dal22
]
and caused by our dominant contribution to growth rates coming from shapes
\Delta
with non-trivial
\mathcal{S}_{\Delta}
.

12.1.3.
Example 3

We will also consider an example where there is only one non-compact place. Assume:

•

\deg F/\mathbb{Q}=d
with a fixed place
v_{0}\in\infty
.

•

G_{\infty}\simeq U(p,q)\times U(N,0)^{d-1}
where

q\equiv\begin{cases}0\pmod{2}&d\text{ even or }N\equiv 0,1\pmod{4}\\
1\pmod{2}&d\text{ odd and }N\equiv 2,3\pmod{4}\end{cases}

to satisfy the conditions of §
2.1
. The
U(p,q)
factor is at
v_{0}
.

•

\pi_{\infty}=\pi_{0}\times\mathbf{1}^{d-1}
where
\mathbf{1}
is the trivial representation and

\pi_{0}=((p_{i},q_{i}))_{i},\qquad n_{i}=p_{i}+q_{i}.

is odd GSK-maxed with the same infinitesimal character as
\mathbf{1}
.

•

S_{1}=\emptyset
.

We need some more combinatorial parameters

•

d_{j}
for
1\leq i\leq k
are the distinct non-
1
values among the
n_{i}
,

•

If
d_{j}=n_{i(d_{j})}
, let
r_{j}=i(d_{j})-\#\{i(d_{j^{\prime}})<i(d_{j})\}

•

M=N-\sum_{j}d_{j}
.

The
\Delta\in\Delta^{\max}(\pi_{\infty})
are then all of the form

(M,1,(\lambda_{v})_{v},\eta),(1,d_{j},(\lambda_{j,v})_{j},\eta_{j})_{j},

where all the
\lambda_{j,v}
are infinitesimal characters of
1
-dimensional irreps and
\lambda_{v_{0}}
is a character twist of

\rho_{M}+\sum_{j}d_{j}\lambda_{r_{j}}.

The possible choices for each
\lambda_{v}
with
v\neq v_{0}
are in bijection with reorderings
(n_{v,i})_{i}
of
(n_{i})_{i}
. We can define
i_{v}(d_{j})
and
r_{v,j}
from
(n_{v,i})_{i}
similar to the original definitions of
i(d_{j})
and
r_{j}
. Then
\lambda_{v}
is equal to a character twist of

\rho_{M}+\sum_{j}d_{j}\lambda_{r_{v,j}}.

The condition on the character from Lemma
11.3.1
then reduces to

(12.1.4)

i(d_{j})+\sum_{v\neq v_{0}}i_{v}(d_{j})\equiv d(\chi_{4}(d_{j})-1)+q_{i(d_{j})}\pmod{2}

for all
1\leq j\leq k
. There is always a set of reorderings
(n_{v,i})_{i}
that satisfy (
12.1.4
): the only way there couldn’t be is if the
d_{j}
are all the
n_{i}
and a parity condition from summing (
12.1.4
) over all
j
fails
6
6

6

This statement boils down to a combinatorial puzzle about filling in a
(\#\{n_{i}\}-1)\times k
grid of squares black or white such that each row has
\lfloor(\#\{n_{i}\}-1)/2\rfloor
black squares and the first
d-1
columns have a fixed parity of black squares.
. However, the parity condition on
q
implies that the condition from summing always holds.

It follows that this is analogous to Example 1 in that we do not need to check the character condition form Lemma
11.3.2
to get lower bounds. In particular the asymptotic (
12.1.1
) holds with a much more complicated factor replacing the
N^{-d}\dim(\pi_{r\lambda})^{d}
.

12.2.
Growth of Cohomology

Our limit multiplicities can be translated into computations of upper and lower bounds for the growth cohomology of arithmetic lattices in
G_{\infty}
. Recall that for a cocompact lattice
\Gamma
in a Lie group
G_{\infty}
with maximal compact
K_{\infty}
and Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, Matsushima
[
Mat67
]
computed the cohomology of
\Gamma
with coefficients in the finite-dimensional representation
F
of
G
:

(12.2.1)

H^{*}(\Gamma,F)=\sum_{\pi\in G_{\infty}^{\vee}}m(\pi,\Gamma)H^{*}(\mathfrak{g},K_{\infty};\pi\otimes F^{*}).

Here
G_{\infty}^{\vee}
is the unitary dual of
G_{\infty}
, the integer
m(\pi,\Gamma)
is the multiplicity of
\pi
in
L^{2}(\Gamma\backslash G_{\infty})
, and
H^{*}(\mathfrak{g},K_{\infty})
is the
(\mathfrak{g},K)
-cohomology of
\pi\otimes F^{*}
; see
[
BW00
]
. We say
\pi
is
cohomological with coefficients in
F
if
H^{*}(\mathfrak{g},K_{\infty};\pi\otimes F^{*})
is nontrivial.

We will restrict our groups
G
so that
G_{\infty}=U(p,q)\times U(N,0)^{a}\times U(0,N)^{b}.
Following Lemma
2.1.1
, such
G
exist for all values of
N
, though possibly not over all unramified extensions
E/F
. We will abuse terminology and say that a degree
i
of cohomology appears in an
A
-packet
\Pi_{\psi}
if it contains a representation with non-vanishing cohomology in degree
i
.

12.2.1.
Lower bounds

We give a sample application of Theorem
11.4.1
to lower bounds on growth of cohomology. First, as a direct consequence of Lemma
11.1.5
and of §
11.1.3
, we find:

Lemma 12.2.1
.

Let
G_{v}=U(p,q)
with
p+q=N
and
\min(p,q)=r
. Let
1<d\leq N
be odd.

(i)

Let
Q\in\mathcal{P}(N)
be an ordered partition with one entry equal to
d
and all others equal to
1
.
Then the lowest degree of cohomology appearing in the packet
\Pi_{\psi_{Q},v}
is:

i=i(d,N,r)=\begin{cases}r(N-r)-\frac{d^{2}-1}{4}&r\geq\frac{d-1}{2}\\
r(N-d)&r\leq\frac{d-1}{2}.\end{cases}

(ii)

If
r\leq\frac{d-1}{2}
, the degree
i
is achieved by a unique
\pi_{i}\in\Pi_{\psi_{Q},v}
. If

Q=(\overbrace{1,\dotsc,1}^{s},d,1,\dotsc,1)\in\mathcal{P}(N),

then the Hodge weight of
\pi_{i}
in degree
i=r(N-d)
is:

(a,b)=\begin{cases}(rs,r(N-d-s))&G=U(N-r,r)\\
(r(N-d-s),rs)&G=U(r,N-r).\end{cases}

From this we deduce the following:

Theorem 12.2.2
.

Let
E/F
be unramified at all finite places, and let
G
be an extended pure inner form of
G^{*}
unramified at all finite places, isomorphic to
U(p,q)
at one infinite place
v_{0}
, and compact at all the others.
For
\mathfrak{n}
divisible only by primes that split in
E
, let
\Gamma(\mathfrak{n})=G(F)\cap K^{G}(\mathfrak{n})
be a lattice in
U(p,q)
of level
\mathfrak{n}
.
Let
N=p+q
and
r=\min(p,q)
. Let
j\not\equiv N\mod 2
be such that
j\leq|p-q|-1
. Then

\dim H^{rj}(\Gamma(\mathfrak{n}),\mathbb{C})\gg|\mathfrak{n}|^{Nj}.

The same bound holds for each
H^{rk,r(j-k)}(\Gamma(\mathfrak{n}),\mathbb{C})
for
0\leq k\leq j.

Proof.

By Lemma
12.2.1
, it suffices to give lower bounds on multiplicities for the representation
\pi_{rj}=\pi_{rj,v_{0}}\otimes{\bf 1}^{[F:\mathbb{Q}]-1}
. Since
\pi_{rj}
is odd GSK-maxed by the congruence condition on
j
, Theorem
11.4.1
gives exact multiplicities with
R=Nj+1
provided that there exists
\Delta\in\Delta(\pi_{0})
with

\eta_{\pi_{rj}}^{\psi_{\infty}^{\Delta}}(\mathcal{S}_{\Delta})\equiv 1.

Since we are in the setup of Example
12.1.3
, there automatically exists such a
\Delta
.

This gives a lower bound for the cohomology of the disconnected locally symmetric spaces
Y(\mathfrak{n})=G(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A})/K(\mathfrak{n})K_{\infty}
, of which
\Gamma(\mathfrak{n})\backslash G_{\infty}/K_{\infty}
is one connected component. Though the different connected components of
Y(\mathfrak{n})
are not necessarily isomorphic, their cohomology is equidimensional by Lemma
12.2.3
below. It then follows from
[
Del71
, §2]
that

\dim H^{i}(Y(\mathfrak{n}),\mathbb{C})=|\pi_{0}(T(F)\backslash T(\mathbb{A})/\nu(K_{\infty}K(\mathfrak{n})))|\dim H^{i}(X(\mathfrak{n}),\mathbb{C}),

for
T=G/G^{\mathrm{der}}
. Since
|\pi_{0}(T(\mathbb{A})/T(F)\nu(K_{\infty}K(\mathfrak{n})))|\gg|\mathfrak{n}|^{1-\epsilon}
, we conclude.
∎

Lemma 12.2.3
.

Let
G^{*}
be an inner form of
G=U_{E/F}(N)
and
K
be a compact open subgroup of
G(\mathbb{A}^{\infty})
. Let

Y_{K}=G(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A})/K_{\infty}K

be the locally symmetric space of level
K
. Let
X_{K,\gamma}
denote the connected components of
Y_{K}
, indexed by representatives of
G(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A}^{\infty})/K
. Then for any
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}
we have

\dim H^{i}(X_{K,\gamma},\mathbb{C})=\dim H^{i}(X_{K,\gamma^{\prime}},\mathbb{C}).

Proof.

Let
G^{\prime}
be the derived subgroup of
G
with
T=G/G^{\prime}
and quotient map
\nu
. We have

Y_{K}=\bigsqcup X_{K,\gamma}:=\bigsqcup_{\gamma}G^{\prime}(F)\backslash G^{\prime}(\mathbb{A})/\gamma K\gamma^{-1}(K_{\infty}\cap G^{\prime}(F_{\infty}))

for a finite set of representatives
\gamma
of
G(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A})/K
. Consider the space
